Groupoid intertwiner and twist for dynamical Yang–Baxter equation: part I
Muze Ren

Abstract
. Intertwiner is a homomorphism between two existing dynamical R ˇ \check{R} matrices, first introduced by Baxter in eight vertex-SOS correspondence, we develop certain equivalence relations among R ˇ \check{R} matrices using intertwiners. Twist is a homomorphism that twist a dynamical R ˇ \check{R} matrix to get a new dynamical R ˇ \check{R} matrix, we introduce a kind of notion of twist that generalize classical Drinfeld twist in quasi-triangular Hopf algebra and some dynamical twist. As applications, we obtain some examples of twists from Ocneanu cell calculus and Fendley–Ginsparg orbifold constructions. The relations between intertwiner and twist are also discussed, the groupoid structures are emphasized.



1.
Introduction

1.1.
Groupoid graded vector space

The dynamical Yang–Baxter equation (
1
) was initially considered by Gervais and Neveu in the study of Liouville theory
[
31
]
. And later Felder in
[
24
,
25
]
rediscovered this equation and the classical dynamical Yang–Baxter equation and found the theory of elliptic quantum group and elliptic R matrix corresponding to the eight vertex SOS model
[
2
]
. Later the equations were widely studied in different aspects and have many connections to other fields, see for examples
[
29
,
27
,
28
,
19
,
18
,
20
,
1
,
8
,
9
,
47
]
, also the standard books
[
17
,
16
,
36
]
and reference there.

\begin{split}&\check{R}^{(23)}(z-w,ah^{(1)})\check{R}^{(12)}(z,a)\check{R}^{(23)}(w,ah^{(1)})\\
&=\check{R}^{(12)}(w,a)\check{R}^{(23)}(z,ah^{(1)})\check{R}^{(12)}(z-w,a)\end{split}

(1)

There are many different types of dynamical
\check{R}(z,\lambda)
matrices. With respect to the spectral parameters, we can roughly divided into the following type

•

Rational

•

Trigonometric

•

Elliptic

And with respect to the dynamical parameters
\lambda
, we can roughly divided them into the following

•

If
\lambda
is trivial, these are the classical quantum group, see for example the book
[
37
,
7
,
34
]
.

•

If
\lambda
is unrestricted, this is the Elliptic quantum group and its various trigonometric and rational degeneration, for examples
[
24
,
25
]
.

•

If
\lambda
is restricted to a finite set, this is the restricted quantum group, for examples in
[
26
]
.

These different types of solutions of dynamical Yang–Baxter equation can be seen as the representations of the usual Yang–Baxter equation (
2
) on different types of vector spaces

\check{R}^{(23)}(z-w)\check{R}^{(12)}(z)\check{R}^{(23)}(w)=\check{R}^{(12)}(w)\check{R}^{(23)}(z)\check{R}^{(12)}(z-w)

(2)

(1)

If we use the usual vector spaces, there are certain elliptic solutions can not appear.

(2)

If we use the
\Z
graded or
\Z^{n}
graded (for example some weight lattices) vector spaces, then more elliptic solutions appears, but not restricted ones.

(3)

If we use the groupoid graded vector spaces which means more diverse and subtle gradings, then the restricted type solutions also appears.

From the above perspectives, groupoid vector spaces language developed in
[
26
]
is a tool to study the general types of
\check{R}(z,\lambda)
matrices.

See also the papers of Ocneanu
[
38
,
39
]
, Goodman–Harpe–Jones
[
32
]
, Pasquier
[
40
,
42
,
41
]
, Pasquier–Saleur
[
43
]
, Roche
[
46
]
, Francesco–Zuber
[
11
]
and Kawahigashi
[
35
]
from the perspective of path model of Temperley–Lieb algebra or the string algebras side to study the integrable system in statistic mechanics, which is more ”global” in the sense of discussion of trace and boundary conditions and also many of them are without spectral parameters. And dynamical Yang-Baxter equation perspective is more local. The work
[
45
]
is also try to understand the local nature of above works.

1.2.
Intertwiner

Intertwiner is a homomorphism which relates two different dynamical
\check{R}
matrices. The concept of intertwiner that we studied here was first introduced by Baxter in
[
6
]
, where he shows the equivalence between ”zero field” 8 vertex model and a SOS model, the equivalence here means that with carefully chosen parameters and boundary conditions the partition function of these two models are the same.

Using the groupoid graded vector space language, it is equivalent to say that there exists an intertwiner
C
such that we have the following relation (
3
) and we take
\check{R}_{1}
to be the dynamical
\check{R}
matrix
\check{R}^{\text{8v}}
of zero field 8 vertex model, take
\check{R}_{2}
to be the dynamical
\check{R}
matrix correspinding to the SOS model
\check{R}^{\text{sos}}
.

C(w)^{(12)}C(z)^{(23)}\check{R}^{\text{8v}}_{1}(z-w)^{(12)}=\check{R}^{\text{sos}}_{2}(z-w)^{(23)}C^{(12)}(z)C^{(23)}(w)

(3)

For physics considerations, the transfer matrix is more interesting, taking the partial trace, we have the relations between ”transfer matrix”,
*_{\pi_{1}},*_{\pi_{2}}
are certain convolution product.

\overline{\operatorname{tr}}_{V^{\pi_{1}}}\check{R}_{1}(z)*_{\pi_{1}}\overline{\operatorname{tr}}_{V^{\pi}}C(z^{\prime})=\overline{\operatorname{tr}}_{V^{\pi}}C(z^{\prime})*_{\pi_{2}}\overline{\operatorname{tr}}_{V^{\pi_{2}}}\check{R}_{2}(z).

(4)

In the situations that
\check{R}_{1},\check{R}_{2}
are indeed usual matrices, from this equation, we know that some ”eigenvalues” and ”eigenvectors” of
\check{R_{1}}
and
\check{R_{2}}
are the same, which will implies certain equivalence of the partition function.

Relation with quantum groups.

The basic
RLL
algebraic formulae of the quantum inverse problem method developed
[
22
,
23
]
by Faddeev, Reshetikhin, Takhtadzhyan can be got by taking the
\check{R}_{1}=\check{R}_{2}
in (
4
) and we use
L
for
C
, we will have

L(w)^{(12)}L(z)^{(23)}\check{R}(z-w)^{(12)}(z)=\check{R}(z-w)^{(23)}L^{(12)}(z)L^{(23)}(w)

(5)

Relation with fusion operations
.

Suppose that we have an (
\check{R}_{1}
,
\check{R}_{2}
) intertwiner
C
and (
\check{R}_{2},\check{R}_{3}
) intertwiner
\widehat{C}
, their composition
\widehat{C}^{(23)}C^{(12)}
is an (
\check{R}_{1},\check{R}_{3}
) intertwiner which means that

\widehat{C}^{(23)}C^{(12)}\widehat{C}^{(34)}C^{(23)}\check{R}_{1}(z)^{(12)}=\check{R}_{3}(z)^{(34)}\widehat{C}^{(23)}C^{(12)}\widehat{C}^{(34)}C^{(23)}

(6)

Various fusion construction in statistic mechanics, for example
[
10
]
and also the coproduct operations constructed in (
5
) which leads to the Hopf algebra formalism can be seen as certain applications of this fact.

Equivalence relation

In the above, we discuss the composition property of intertwiners, we can also consider the tranpose relation to make the existence of intertwiners an equivalence relation. We define the transpose of the
\check{R}
matrix and the transpose of an intertwiner, which means taking the inverse directions from
\check{R}_{2}
to
\check{R}_{1}
. And with the assumption that
\check{R}_{1}=\check{R}^{T}_{1}
and
\check{R}^{T}_{2}=\check{R}_{2}
, this is an equivalence relation. If we further assume that the intertwiners are invertible, we still have an equivalence relation, we call it the transfer matrices equivalence.

From the perspective of conformal field theory and integrable lattice models, the intertwiner are also widely studied, see for examples the work of Ocneanu
[
38
,
39
]
, Pasquier
[
40
,
42
,
41
]
, Pasquier–Saleur
[
43
]
, Roche
[
46
]
, Francesco–Zuber
[
11
]
and Pearce–Zhou
[
44
]
.

In the fundamental work of Baxter
[
6
]
, actually the intertwiners and the
\check{R}^{\text{sos}}
are all constructed. Implicitly it suggested that from an existing
\check{R}
matrices and intertwiners, based on some properties of intertwiner, we could build a new dynamical
\check{R}
matrix. And one of the motivation to study the twist is to understand what conditions we need have, in order to have a new
\check{R}
matrix that satisfies the dynamical Yang–Baxter equation.

1.3.
Twist

Twist is a homomorphism which transform one dynamical
\check{R}
matrix to get a new dynamical
\check{R}
matrix.

Twist non-dynamical
\check{R}
matrices to non-dynamical
\check{R}
matrices

The first step of the theory of twist is to twist a non-dynamical
\check{R}
matrix to a non-dynamical
\check{R}
matrix. In the theory of quasi-triangular quasi-Hopf algebra
[
14
]
, Drinfeld introduced this kind notion of twist.
See also the notion of fiber functors in category theory, see chapter 5 of the book
[
15
]
and the historical remarks and reference there.

For a quasitriangular quasi-Hopf algebra
(A,\Delta,\epsilon,\Phi,R)
, let
J
be an invertible element of
A\otimes A
such that
(\id\otimes\epsilon)(J)=1=(\epsilon\otimes\id)(J)
, the equation (1.10), (1.11) and (1.12) in
[
14
]
are

\widetilde{\Delta}(a)=J\Delta(a)J^{-1},

(7a)

\widetilde{\Phi}=J^{(23)}(\id\otimes\Delta)(J)\Phi(\Delta\otimes\id)(J^{-1})(J^{12})^{-1},

(7b)

\widetilde{R}=J^{21}RJ^{-1},

(7c)

we obtain a new quasitriangular quasi-Hopf algebra
(A,\widetilde{\Delta},\epsilon,\widetilde{\Phi},\widetilde{R})
. If we assume that
\Phi
always be 1, we goes to the quasi-triangular Hopf algebra, then in order to let
\widetilde{\Phi}
be 1, we got the following twist equation of
J
, (2 cocycle equation)

(\Delta\otimes\operatorname{id})(J)(J\otimes 1)=(\operatorname{id}\otimes\Delta)(J)(1\otimes J)

(8)

Twist non-dynamical
\check{R}
matrices to dynamical
\check{R}
matrices

In the work of
[
4
,
5
]
, Babelon, Bernard and Billy generalize the notion of twist that can twist in
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
case, a non-dynamical
\check{R}
matrix to a dynamical
\check{R}
matrix, in this case, they got the dynamical twist equation, the shifted cocycle equation (4) in
[
5
]
.

[(\Delta\otimes\id)J]\cdot[J(xq^{3})\otimes\id]=\big((\id\otimes\Delta)J)\cdot[\id\otimes J]

(9)

and the ”shifted coboundary” equation (5) in
[
5
]
,

J_{12}(x)=\Delta M(x)[\id\otimes M(x)]^{-1}[M(xq^{H_{2}})\otimes\id]^{-1}.

(10)

Using this formalism, there are many important results, for example the Arnaudon–Buffenoir–Rogoucy–Roche equation
[
3
]
was found and also
[
33
]
Jimbo–Konno–Odake–Shiraishi twistor between the elliptic vertex type and face type
\check{R}
matrices. For the general case beyond
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
, they were done by Etingof–Varchenko
[
20
]
.

Twist dynamical
\check{R}
matrices to dynamical
\check{R}
matrices

In order to twist dynamical
\check{R}
matrices to dynamical
\check{R}
matrices based on the groupoid graded vector spaces language, we need to find the twist equations which generalize the equation (
8
) and (
9
).

We first reformulate (
8
), an invertible operator
J:V\otimes V\to V\otimes V
is a twist if there exists an invertible operator
Q\in\End(V\otimes V\otimes V)
such that

\begin{split}&\check{R}_{J}=J^{-1}\check{R}J\\
&\check{R}_{J}\otimes\operatorname{id}=Q(\check{R}\otimes\operatorname{id})Q^{-1}\\
&\operatorname{id}\otimes\check{R}_{J}=Q(\operatorname{id}\otimes\check{R})Q^{-1}\\
\end{split}

(11)

The relation between (
8
) and (
11
) is in the section
5
. Similarly, we reformulate the equation (
9
) as the following (
12
).

\displaystyle\check{R}_{J}=J^{-1}\check{R}J

(12)

\displaystyle\check{R}_{J}(\lambda)\otimes\operatorname{id}=Q(\lambda)(\check{R}\otimes\operatorname{id})Q^{-1}(\lambda)

\displaystyle\operatorname{id}\otimes\check{R}_{J}(\lambda-h^{1})=Q(\lambda)(\operatorname{id}\otimes\check{R})Q^{-1}(\lambda)

The relation between (
11
) and (
12
) is in
the section
6
.

One obstacle when we want to twist dynamical
\check{R}
matrix on a groupoid
\pi_{1}
to get a new dynamical
\check{R}
matrix on another groupoid
\pi_{2}
is to relate the two different groupoid structures. We first introduce some groupoid
\pi
that connects
\pi_{1}
and
\pi_{2}
, then we introduce the notion of connecting system which are arrows in
\pi
that relate the paths in these two groupoid. We distinguish two cases, the unique connecting system in subsection
4.2
and the quasi-unique connecting system in the subsection
4.3
. For the unique connecting system, the twist equations is similar to (
11
) and (
12
).

For a quasi-unique connecting system and an invertible map
j
, we can define

\check{R}_{2}(\beta_{2},\beta_{1})=j^{-1}*_{\circ}(\check{R_{1}}j):=\sum_{\beta}j^{-1}(\beta,\beta_{2})\check{R}_{1}j(\beta_{1},\beta),

(13)

j
is said to be a twist if it satisfies the following two conditions

•

We have the weight 0 conditions (
14
) for any
\beta_{2}
with
t_{\pi}(\beta_{2})=s_{\pi_{2}}(\gamma)
and any
s_{\pi}(\beta^{\prime}_{2})=s_{\pi}(\beta_{2})
,graphically it is (
112
).

•

The factorization property (
15
) for invertible
q_{1},q_{2},q
and path
\gamma
in
\pi_{2}
, graphically it is (
114
).

\operatorname{id}_{V^{\pi_{2}}}\otimes(\check{R}_{2})_{\lhd}\delta_{\beta_{2},\beta^{\prime}_{2}}=\big(q_{2}^{-1}*_{\circ}\big((\operatorname{id}_{V^{\pi_{1}}}\otimes\check{R}_{1})q_{2}\big)\big)(\beta_{2},\beta^{\prime}_{2})

(14a)

(\check{R}_{2})_{\lhd}\otimes\operatorname{id}_{V^{\pi_{2}}}\delta_{\beta_{2},\beta^{\prime}_{2}}=\big(q_{2}^{-1}*_{\circ}\big((\check{R}_{1}\otimes\operatorname{id}_{V^{\pi_{1}}})q_{2}\big)\big)(\beta_{2},\beta^{\prime}_{2})

(14b)

q|_{(V^{\pi_{2}})^{\otimes n}_{\gamma\gamma^{\prime}}}=q_{1}|_{(V^{\pi_{2}})^{\otimes n-3}_{\gamma^{\prime}}}*_{\otimes}q_{2}|_{(V^{\pi_{2}})^{\otimes 3}_{\gamma}}

(15a)

\operatorname{id}^{\otimes n-2}_{V^{\pi_{2}}}\otimes(\check{R}_{2})_{\lhd}|_{(V^{\pi_{2}})^{\otimes n}_{\gamma\gamma^{\prime}}}=(q^{-1}*_{\circ}\big((\operatorname{id}^{\otimes n-2}_{V^{\pi_{1}}}\otimes\check{R}_{1})q\big))_{\lhd}

(15b)

\id^{\otimes n-3}_{V^{\pi_{2}}}\otimes(\check{R}_{2})_{\lhd}\otimes\operatorname{id}_{V^{\pi_{2}}}|_{(V^{\pi_{2}})^{\otimes n}_{\gamma\gamma^{\prime}}}=(q^{-1}*_{\circ}\big((\id^{\otimes n-3}_{V^{\pi_{1}}}\otimes\check{R}_{1}\otimes\operatorname{id}^{\otimes n-2}_{V^{\pi_{1}}})q\big))_{\lhd}

(15c)

The structure of the paper is organized as follows. In section
2
, we introduce the main setting of groupoid graded vector space, the YBE and dynamical YBE. In section
3
, we first develop the language of modules of convolution algebra in
3.1
in order to express ”relation between transfer matrix” precisely. And then in subsection
3.2
,
3.3
and
3.4
, we develop the equivalence relations and analyze the compatibility relations implied by the existence of intertwiner. In subsection
3.5
and
3.6
, we study the intertwiner with spectral parameters and analyze the example of eight vertex sos intertwiner and the equivalence relation.

In section
4
, we first develop the language of transfer operators and its operations in
4.1
and then define the twist and connecting system in
4.2
and
4.3
. In subsection
4.4
, we discuss the relation between intertwiner and twist.

In section
5
,
6
and
7
, we discuss the examples of different type of twists.

2.
Preliminary

We first recall the groupoid language developed in
[
26
]
, the definition of Yang–Baxter operator and dynamical Yang–Baxter operator in this setting.

2.1.
Groupoid graded vector space

A groupoid
\pi
is a small category in which every morphism is invertible. We denote its object by
\text{Ob}(\pi)
, for any
a,b\in\text{Ob}(\pi)
, the set of morphisms from an object
a
to
b
is denoted by
\pi(a,b)
, the morphisms are also called as arrows, the composition of arrows
\gamma\in\pi(a,b)
and
\eta\in\pi(a,b)
is denoted by
\eta\circ\gamma\in\pi(a,c)
. The object of
\pi
is identified with the identity arrows.

By abuse of notation, the set of arrows of
\pi
is also denoted by
\pi
, then we can denote the source and target maps by
s,t:\pi\to\text{Ob}(\pi)
. For
a\in\rm{Ob}(\pi)
, the source fibers are defined by
s^{-1}(a)
and the target fibers are
t^{-1}(a)
.

Example 2.1
.

For any unoriented graph
\mathcal{G}
, it can be seen as a groupoid
\pi(\mathcal{G})
by taking the vertices as objects, for each unoriented edge
e\in\mathcal{G}
, we have corresponding two inverse direction arrows
\alpha_{e},\alpha^{-1}_{e}\in\pi(\mathcal{G})
.

Example 2.2

(Action groupoid)
.

Let
A
be a set with right group action
A\times G\to A
, the action groupoid
A\rtimes G
has the set of objects
A
and for each
a^{\prime}=ag
, there is an arrow
a\xrightarrow{g}a^{\prime}
, thus an arrow is described by a pair
(a,g)\in A\times G
. The source and target are
s(a,g)=a,t(a,g)=ag
and the composition is

\displaystyle(a^{\prime},g^{\prime})\circ(a,g)=(a,gg^{\prime}),\quad\text{with}~a^{\prime}=ag,

the identity arrows are
(a,e),a\in A
and the inverse of
(a,g)
is
(ag,g^{-1})
.

With groupoids, we can define the groupoid graded vector spaces with certain finite conditions.

Definition 2.3
.

Let
\pi
be a groupoid, a
\pi
-graded vector space of finite type over a field
k
is a collection
(V_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in\pi}
of finite dimensional vector spaces indexed by the arrows of
\pi
, such that for each
a\in\rm{Ob}(\pi)
, there are only finitely many arrows
\alpha
with source or target
a
and nonzero
V_{\alpha}
. For
a\in\rm{Ob}(\pi)
, the
a
source fibers of the vector space
V\in\pi
is the direct sum of components with source
a
, that is
\oplus_{\alpha\in s^{-1}(a)}V_{\alpha}
. And similar for the target fibers.

The
k
vector space
\Hom(V,W)
of two
\pi
graded vector spaces consists of families
(f_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in\pi}
of linear maps
f_{\alpha}:V_{\alpha}\to W_{\alpha}
, that is
f_{\alpha}\in\End_{k}(V)
, the composition is also defined componentwise. The category of
\pi
groupoid graded vector spaces is a monoidal category, we denote by
\text{Vect}_{k}(\pi)
, with the tensor product defined

\displaystyle(V\otimes W)_{\gamma}=\oplus_{\beta\circ\alpha=\gamma}V_{\alpha}\otimes W_{\beta},\quad V,W\in\text{Vect}_{k}(\pi)

For the
k
additive category
\rm{Vect}_{k}(\pi)
, we can also define the dual groupoid graded vector space and the resulting category is an abelian pivotal monoidal category, see section 2 of
[
26
]
.

2.2.
Yang–Baxter operator and its restriction on source fibers

Let
k=\C
, a
Yang-Baxter operator
on
V\in\text{Vect}_{k}(\pi)
is a meromorphic function
z\to\check{R}(z)\in\End(V\otimes V)
of the spectral parameter
z\in\C
with values in the endormorphisms of
V\otimes V
, obeying the Yang–Baxter equation

\displaystyle\check{R}(z-w)^{(23)}\check{R}(z)^{(12)}\check{R}(w)^{(23)}=\check{R}(w)^{(12)}\check{R}(z)^{(23)}\check{R}(z-w)^{(12)}

(16)

and also the inversion relation

\check{R}(z)\check{R}(-z)=\id_{V\otimes V}

(17)

The restriction of
\check{R}(z)
to
V_{\alpha}\otimes V_{\beta}
for composable arrows
\alpha,\beta
has components in each direct summand of the decomposition

\displaystyle\check{R}(z)|_{V_{\alpha}\otimes V_{\beta}}=\oplus_{\gamma,\delta}\mathcal{W}(z;\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\delta),

the sum is over
\gamma,\delta
such that
\beta\circ\alpha=\delta\circ\gamma
with the component
\mathcal{W}(z;\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\delta)\in\Hom_{k}(V_{\alpha}\otimes V_{\beta},V_{\gamma}\otimes V_{\delta})
, graphically it is simply presented as the square (
18
).

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{4}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{3}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V_{\alpha}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{W}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V_{\gamma}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V_{\delta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V^{\beta}}

(18)

When restricting to the source fibers
s^{-1}(a)
of
V\otimes V
,
a\in\text{Ob}(\pi)
, the Yang–Baxter operator
\check{R}(z)
can be written as
\check{R}(z,a)
and

\check{R}(z,a)\in\oplus_{\alpha\in s^{-1}(a)}\End_{k}\big((V\otimes V)_{\alpha}\big),

the Yang–Baxter equation then turns to the following dynamical Yang-Baxter equation

\begin{split}&\check{R}^{(23)}(z-w,ah^{(1)})\check{R}^{(12)}(z,a)\check{R}^{(23)}(w,ah^{(1)})\\
&=\check{R}^{(12)}(w,a)\check{R}^{(23)}(z,ah^{(1)})\check{R}^{(12)}(z-w,a)\end{split}

(19)

here we use the ”dynamical” notation with the placeholder
h^{(i)}
:

\displaystyle\check{R}^{(23)}(ah^{(1)})(u\otimes v\otimes w)=u\otimes\check{R}(t(\alpha_{1}))(v\otimes w),\quad\text{if}\quad u\in V_{\alpha_{1}},s(\alpha_{1})=a

Example 2.4
.

In the case of an action groupoid
\pi=A\rtimes G
and its subgroupoids, the operator can be written explicitly as

\check{R}(z,d)\in\oplus_{g\in G}\End_{k}((V\otimes V)_{(d,g)}),\quad(V\otimes V)_{(d,g)}=\sum_{h\in G}V_{(d,h)}\otimes V_{(dh,h^{-1}g)},

(20)

for any composable edges, we have

\displaystyle\check{R}(z,d)|_{V_{(d,g_{1})}\otimes V_{(a,g_{2})}}=\oplus_{(d,g_{3}),(c,g_{4})}\mathcal{W}\big(x;(d,g_{1}),(a,g_{2}),(d,g_{3}),(c,g_{4})\big),

where
\mathcal{W}\big(x;(d,g_{1}),(a,g_{2}),(d,g_{3}),(c,g_{4})\big)\in\Hom_{k}(V_{(d,g_{1})}\otimes V_{(a,g_{2})},V_{(d,g_{3})}\otimes V_{(c,g_{3})})
, graphically it is the following with
d
in the left upper corner.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V_{(d,g_{3})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V_{(d,g_{1})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V_{(c,g_{4})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V_{(a,g_{2})}}

(21)

3.
Intertwiner

For two groupoids
\pi_{1},\pi_{2}
, suppose that we have a set
\pi
with the map
\operatorname{Ob}(\pi_{1})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle s_{\pi}}}{{\leftarrow}}\pi\stackrel{{\scriptstyle t_{\pi}}}{{\rightarrow}}\operatorname{Ob}(\pi_{2})
, with the source and target map defined, we can view
\pi
as arrows directing from
\pi_{1}
to
\pi_{2}
,

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rm{Ob}(\pi_{1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rm{Ob}(\pi_{2}),}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}

we further assume that
t_{\pi}
and
s_{\pi}
are surjective. The left, right and left-right fiber products are formed respectively by

\pi_{1}\times_{\rm{Ob}(\pi_{1})}\pi:=\{(\alpha,\beta)|t_{\pi_{1}}(\alpha)=s_{\pi}(\beta)\}

(22)

\pi\times_{\rm{Ob}(\pi_{2})}\pi_{2}:=\{(\beta,\gamma)|t_{\pi}(\beta)=s_{\pi_{2}}(\gamma)\}

(23)

\pi_{1}\times_{\rm{Ob}(\pi_{1})}\pi\times_{\rm{Ob}(\pi_{2})}\pi_{2}:=\{(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)|t_{\pi_{1}}(\alpha)=s_{\pi}(\beta),t_{\pi}(\beta)=s_{\pi_{2}}(\gamma)\}

(24)

The left
\pi_{1}
-module generated by the set
\pi
is the set
\pi_{1}\times_{\rm{Ob}(\pi_{1})}\pi
together with the action map

\begin{split}&\pi_{1}*_{\pi_{1}}\big(\pi_{1}\times_{\rm{Ob}(\pi_{1})}\pi\big)\to\big(\pi_{1}\times_{\rm{Ob}(\pi_{1})}\pi\big)\\
&\alpha_{1}*_{\pi_{1}}(\alpha,\beta)\mapsto(\alpha\circ\alpha_{1},\beta),\end{split}

(25)

and similarly definition for the right
\pi_{2}
module structure on
\pi\times_{\rm{Ob}(\pi_{2})}\pi_{2}
and the
(\pi_{1},\pi_{2})
bimodule structure on the set
\pi_{1}\times_{\rm{Ob}(\pi_{1})}\pi\times_{\rm{Ob}(\pi_{2})}\pi_{2}
.

By viewing the object of a groupoid as identity arrow, we also have the following relations

\pi_{1}\times_{\rm{Ob}(\pi_{1})}\pi\subset\pi_{1}\times_{\rm{Ob}(\pi_{1})}\pi\times_{\rm{Ob}(\pi_{2})}\pi_{2}

(26a)

\pi\times_{\rm{Ob}(\pi_{2})}\pi_{2}\subset\pi_{1}\times_{\rm{Ob}(\pi_{1})}\pi\times_{\rm{Ob}(\pi_{2})}\pi_{2}

(26b)

For simplicity of notations, sometimes we use
\pi
to also denote the bimodule
\pi_{1}\times_{\rm{Ob}(\pi_{1})}\pi\times_{\rm{Ob}(\pi_{2})}\pi_{2}
generated by
\pi
, then the bimodule structure will be denoted by
\pi_{1}*_{\pi_{1}}\pi*_{\pi_{2}}\pi_{2}
.

3.1.
Modules of convolution algebra

Definition 3.1

(
[
26
]
)
.

A
\pi_{1}
graded algebra
R
over
k
is a collection
(R_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in\pi_{1}}
of
k
vector spaces labeled by arrows of
\pi_{1}
with bilinear products
R_{\alpha}\times R_{\beta}\to R_{\beta\circ\alpha},(x,y)\to xy
defined for composable arrows
\alpha,\beta
and units
1_{a}\in R_{a}
, for
a\in\operatorname{\pi_{1}}
such that
(i)(xy)z=x(yz)
whenever defined and
(ii)x1_{b}=x=1_{a}x
for all
x\in R_{\alpha}
of degree
\alpha\in\pi_{1}(a,b)
.

The prototype of groupoid graded algebra and the ones that we will use are the following two examples.

Example 3.2
.

Let
V^{\pi_{1}}\in\text{Vect}_{k}(\pi_{1})
and let
\underline{\operatorname{End}}V^{\pi_{1}}
be the
\pi_{1}
graded vector space with
(\underline{\operatorname{End}}V^{\pi_{1}})_{\alpha_{1}}=\oplus_{\alpha\in\pi(a_{1},a_{1})}\operatorname{Hom}(V^{\pi_{1}}_{\alpha_{1}\circ\alpha\circ\alpha_{1}^{-1}},V^{\pi_{1}}_{\alpha})
where
a_{1}=s(\alpha_{1})
. Let
\alpha^{\prime}=\alpha_{1}\alpha\alpha_{1}^{-1}
and
\alpha^{\prime\prime}=\alpha_{2}\alpha_{1}\alpha\alpha_{1}^{-1}\alpha_{2}^{-1}
, then the product of
\underline{\operatorname{End}}V^{\pi_{1}}
is given by the composition of linear maps

\operatorname{Hom}_{k}(V^{\pi_{1}}_{\alpha^{\prime}},V^{\pi_{1}}_{\alpha})\otimes\operatorname{Hom}_{k}(V^{\pi_{1}}_{\alpha^{\prime\prime}},V^{\pi_{1}}_{\alpha^{\prime}})\to\operatorname{Hom}_{k}(V^{\pi_{1}}_{\alpha^{\prime\prime}},V^{\pi_{1}}_{\alpha}),

(27)

the equation (
27
) graphically can be described by (
28
) and the direction of the maps is from bottom to top.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{3}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{3}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V^{\pi_{1}}_{\alpha}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V^{\pi_{1}}_{\alpha^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V^{\pi_{1}}_{\alpha^{\prime\prime}}}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rm{direction}}

(28)

and unit
1_{\alpha_{1}}=\oplus_{\alpha\in\pi(a_{1},a_{1})}\operatorname{id}_{V_{\alpha}}
is a
\pi_{1}
graded algebra.

Example 3.3
.

Let
V^{\pi_{1}}\in\text{Vect}_{k}(\pi_{1})
and let
\overline{\End}V^{\pi_{1}}
be the
\pi_{1}
graded vector space with
(\overline{\End})_{\alpha_{1}}=\oplus_{\alpha\in\pi(a_{1},a_{1})}\Hom(V^{\pi_{1}}_{\alpha},V^{\pi_{1}}_{\alpha_{1}\circ\alpha\circ\alpha^{-1}_{1}})
, where
a_{1}=s(\alpha_{1})
. Let
\alpha^{\prime}=\alpha_{1}\circ\alpha\circ\alpha^{-1}_{1}
and
\alpha^{\prime\prime}=\alpha_{2}\alpha_{1}\alpha\alpha^{-1}_{1}\alpha^{-1}_{2}
, then the product of
\overline{\End}V^{\pi_{1}}
is given by the composition of linear maps

\Hom(V^{\pi_{1}}_{\alpha^{\prime}},V^{\pi_{1}}_{\alpha^{\prime\prime}})\otimes\Hom_{k}(V^{\pi_{1}}_{\alpha},V^{\pi_{1}}_{\alpha^{\prime}})\to\Hom_{k}(V^{\pi_{1}}_{\alpha},V^{\pi_{1}}_{\alpha^{\prime\prime}})

(29)

and the equation (
29
) can be described graphically as (
30
) and the direction is horizontal from left to right.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{3}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{3}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V^{\pi_{1}}_{\alpha}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V^{\pi_{1}}_{\alpha^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V^{\pi_{1}}_{\alpha^{\prime\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rm{direction}}

(30)

Definition 3.4
.

Let
R
be a
\pi_{1}
graded algebra over
k
, an
R
module over
\pi
is a collection
(Q_{\beta})_{\beta\in\pi}
labeled by
\beta\in\pi
with bilinear maps
R_{\alpha}\times Q_{\beta}\to Q_{\alpha{*_{\pi_{1}}}\beta},(x,q)\to x*_{\pi_{1}}q
and satisfies the relation
(i)x(yq)=(xy)q
and
1_{a}q=q
for all
q\in Q_{\beta}
with
s_{\pi}(\beta)=a
.

Here we make a little abuse of notations
*_{\pi_{1}}
to denote both the module structure of the set
\pi
and the graded module
Q
.

Example 3.5
.

Let
V^{\pi_{1}}\in\text{Vect}_{k}(\pi_{1})

V^{\pi_{2}}\in\text{Vect}_{k}(\pi_{2})
and we use
\overline{\operatorname{Hom}}(V^{\pi_{1}},V^{\pi_{2}})
denote the
\pi
graded vector space,
\overline{\operatorname{Hom}}(V^{\pi_{1}},V^{\pi_{2}})_{\beta}=\oplus_{\alpha,\gamma}\operatorname{Hom}(V^{\pi_{1}}_{\alpha},V^{\pi_{2}}_{\gamma})
, with
\alpha\in\pi_{1}(s_{\pi}(\beta),s_{\pi}(\beta)),\gamma\in\pi_{2}(t_{\pi}(\beta),t_{\pi}(\beta))
, graphically it is described by

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V^{\pi_{1}}_{\alpha}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V^{\pi_{2}}_{\gamma}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}

(31)

The graded vector space
\overline{\operatorname{Hom}}(V^{\pi_{1}},V^{\pi_{2}})
is an
\big(\overline{\operatorname{End}}(V^{\pi_{1}}),\overline{\operatorname{End}}(V^{\pi_{2}})\big)
bimodule and the bimodule structure is easily described by the picture

(33)

More generally, it can be graded by two arrows of
\pi
,
\overline{\operatorname{Hom}}(V^{\pi_{1}},V^{\pi_{2}})_{\beta_{1},\beta_{2}}=\oplus_{\alpha,\gamma}\operatorname{Hom}(V^{\pi_{1}}_{\alpha},V^{\pi_{2}}_{\gamma})
, with
\alpha\in\pi_{1}(s_{\pi}(\beta_{2}),s_{\pi}(\beta_{1})),\gamma\in\pi_{2}(t_{\pi}(\beta_{2}),t(\pi)(\beta_{1}))
, and graphically it is

\overline{\operatorname{Hom}}(V^{\pi_{2}},V^{\pi_{1}})
, it is graphically

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V^{\pi_{2}}_{\gamma}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V^{\pi_{1}}_{\alpha}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta_{2}}

(35)

and also transfer operators of various tensor products of vector spaces.

Definition 3.6
.

[
26
]

Let
R
be a
\pi_{1}
graded algebra, the convolution algebra
\Gamma(\pi_{1},R)
with coefficients in
R
is the
k
algebra of maps
f:\pi_{1}\to\sqcup_{\alpha\in\pi}R_{\alpha}
such that

(1)

f(\alpha)\in R_{\alpha}
for all arrows
\alpha\in\pi_{1}
,

(2)

for every
a\in A
, there are finitely many
\alpha\in s^{-1}(a)\cup t^{-1}(a)
such that
f(\alpha)\neq 0
.

The product of the convolution algebra is the convolution product

g*f(\alpha)=\sum_{\alpha_{2}\circ\alpha_{1}=\alpha}f(\alpha_{2})g(\alpha_{1})

(36)

Definition 3.7
.

Let
Q
be an
R
module, the
\Gamma(\pi_{1},R)
module
\Gamma(\pi,Q)
with coefficients in
Q
is the
k
algebra of maps
l:\pi\to\sqcup_{\beta\in\pi}Q_{\beta}
such that

(1)

l(\beta)
in
Q_{\beta}
for all arrows
\beta\in\pi
.

(2)

for every
a\in\operatorname{Ob}(\pi_{1})
, there are finitely many
\beta\in s^{-1}_{\pi}(a)
such that
l(\beta)\neq 0
.

The
\Gamma(\pi_{1},R)
module structure of
\Gamma(\pi,Q)
structure is the following,

f*_{\pi_{1}}l(\beta)=\sum_{\alpha^{\prime}*_{\pi_{1}}\beta^{\prime}=\beta}l(\beta^{\prime})f(\alpha^{\prime})

(37)

Similarly, we can define the bimodules of two convolution algebras.

3.2.
Intertwiner between Yang–Baxter operators

Definition 3.8
.

Suppose that
\check{R}_{1}(z)\in\operatorname{End}(V^{\pi_{1}}\otimes_{\pi_{1}}V^{\pi_{1}})
and
\check{R}_{2}(z)\in\operatorname{End}(V^{\pi_{2}}\otimes_{\pi_{2}}V^{\pi_{2}})
are two Yang–Baxter operators on the space of
V^{\pi_{1}}
and
V^{\pi_{2}}
, a map
C\in\operatorname{Hom}(V^{\pi_{1}}\otimes_{*\pi_{1}}V^{\pi},V^{\pi}\otimes_{*\pi_{2}}V^{\pi_{2}})
is called an
(\check{R_{1}},\check{R_{2}})
intertwiner if it satisfies the following RCC relation

C^{(12)}C^{(23)}\check{R}^{(12)}_{1}(z)=\check{R}^{(23)}_{2}(z)C^{(12)}C^{(23)}

(38)

in the space
\operatorname{Hom}(V^{\pi_{1}}\otimes_{\pi_{1}}V^{\pi_{1}}\otimes_{*\pi_{1}}V^{\pi},V^{\pi}\otimes_{*\pi_{2}}V^{\pi_{2}}\otimes_{\pi_{2}}V^{\pi_{2}})
.

Now we consider the composition of intertwiners, suppose that we have another groupoid
\rm{Ob}(\pi_{2})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle s_{\hat{\pi}}}}{{\leftarrow}}\hat{\pi}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle t_{\hat{\pi}}}}{{\rightarrow}}\rm{Ob}(\pi_{3})
. Let
\widehat{C}\in\Hom(V^{\pi_{2}}\otimes_{*_{\pi_{2}}}V^{\hat{\pi}},V^{\hat{\pi}}\otimes_{*_{\pi_{3}}}V^{\pi_{3}})
be an
(\check{R}_{2},\check{R}_{3})
intertwiner,which satisfies the relation

\widehat{C}^{(12)}\widehat{C}^{(23)}\check{R}^{(12)}_{2}(z)=\check{R}^{(23)}_{3}(z)\widehat{C}^{(12)}\widehat{C}^{(23)}.

(39)

The composition of two groupoids
\hat{\pi}\circ\pi
connects the groupoid
\pi_{1}
and
\pi_{3}
and also the homomorphism
\widehat{C}^{23}C^{12}\in\Hom(V^{\pi_{1}}\otimes V^{\pi}\otimes V^{\hat{\pi}},V^{\pi}\otimes V^{\hat{\pi}}\otimes V^{\pi_{3}})

\rm{Ob}(\pi_{1})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle s_{\hat{\pi}\circ\pi}}}{{\leftarrow}}\hat{\pi}\circ\pi\stackrel{{\scriptstyle t_{\hat{\pi}\circ\pi}}}{{\rightarrow}}\rm{Ob}(\pi_{3})

(40)

Proposition 3.9
.

\widehat{C}^{(23)}C^{(12)}
is an
(\check{R}_{1},\check{R}_{3})
intertwiner, it satisfies the equation

\widehat{C}^{(23)}C^{(12)}\widehat{C}^{(34)}C^{(23)}\check{R}_{1}(z)^{(12)}=\check{R}_{3}(z)^{(34)}\widehat{C}^{(23)}C^{(12)}\widehat{C}^{(34)}C^{(23)}

(41)

From the intertwiner, we can construct some modules of convolution algebra, we first discuss some inversion relations. The map
C\in\operatorname{Hom}(V^{\pi_{1}}\otimes_{*\pi_{1}}V^{\pi},V^{\pi}\otimes_{*\pi_{2}}V^{\pi_{2}})
is called
left invertible
if there exists a map
C_{l}^{-1}\in\operatorname{Hom}(V^{\pi}\otimes_{*_{\pi_{2}}}V^{\pi_{2}},V^{\pi_{1}}\otimes_{*\pi_{1}}V^{\pi})
such that

C_{l}^{-1}C=\operatorname{id}_{V^{\pi_{1}}\otimes V^{\pi}}\in\operatorname{Hom}(V^{\pi_{1}}\otimes_{*\pi_{1}}V^{\pi},V^{\pi_{1}}\otimes_{*\pi_{1}}V^{\pi})

(42)

and graphically it is presented as (
43
) and notice the convention for the order of composition of the block,

(43)

And
C
is called
right invertible
if there exists
C^{-1}_{r}\in\Hom(V^{\pi}\otimes_{*\pi_{2}}V^{\pi_{2}},V^{\pi_{1}}\otimes_{*\pi})
that satisfies the equation

CC_{r}^{-1}=\operatorname{id}_{V^{\pi}\otimes V^{\pi_{2}}}\in\operatorname{Hom}(V^{\pi}\otimes_{*\pi_{2}}V^{\pi_{2}},V^{\pi}\otimes_{*\pi_{2}}V^{\pi_{2}})

(44)

and graphically the equation (
44
) is presented as

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}

(45)

And
C
is called
invertible
if it is both left (
42
) and right invertible (
44
) and also

C^{-1}:=C^{-1}_{l}=C^{-1}_{r}

(46)

Now suppose that we have an
(\check{R}_{1}(z),V^{\pi_{1}}),(\check{R}_{2}(z),V^{\pi_{2}})
intertwiner
C
, then its partial trace
\overline{\operatorname{tr}}_{V^{\pi}}C\in\Gamma(\pi,\overline{\operatorname{Hom}}(V^{\pi_{1}},V^{\pi_{2}}))
is defined as follows.

Definition 3.10
.

For each
\beta\in\pi(a,b),\gamma\in\pi_{2}(b,b),\alpha\in\pi_{1}(a,a)
, the homogeneous component of
C
is the linear map

C(\beta,\alpha,\gamma):V^{\pi_{1}}_{\alpha}\otimes V^{\pi}_{\beta}\to V^{\pi}_{\beta}\otimes V^{\pi_{2}}_{\gamma},

define
\overline{\tr}_{V^{\pi}}C(\beta,\alpha,\gamma)=\sum_{i}(e^{*}_{i,\beta}\otimes\id)C(\beta,\alpha,\gamma)(\operatorname{id}\otimes e_{i,\beta})\in\operatorname{Hom}(V^{\pi_{1}}_{\alpha},V^{\pi_{2}}_{\gamma})
, for the chosen basis
e_{i,\beta}
of
V^{\pi}_{\beta}
, the partial trace is defined by

\overline{\tr}_{V^{\pi}}C:\beta\to\oplus_{\gamma\in\pi_{2}(b,b),\alpha\in\pi_{1}(a,a)}\operatorname{tr}_{V^{\pi}}C(\beta,\alpha,\gamma)

(47)

Example 3.11
.

If we set
\pi=\pi_{2}=\pi_{1}
and
\check{R}_{1}\in\End(V^{\pi_{1}})
, then we have

\overline{\tr}_{V^{\pi_{1}}}\check{R}_{1}\in\Gamma(\pi_{1},\overline{\End}(V^{\pi_{1}}))

(48)

and similarly if we take the trace over the first coordinate, then we will have

\underline{\tr}_{V^{\pi_{1}}}\check{R}_{1}\in\Gamma(\pi_{1},\underline{\End}(V^{\pi_{1}}))

(49)

Proposition 3.12
.

Suppose that
C
is an
(\check{R_{1}},\check{R_{2}})
intertwiner and is invertible, then we have the following relation (
50
) in the space
\Gamma(\pi,\overline{\Hom}(V^{\pi_{1}},V^{\pi_{2}}))

\overline{\tr}_{V^{\pi_{1}}}\check{R}_{1}*_{\pi_{1}}\overline{\tr}_{V^{\pi}}C=\overline{\tr}_{V^{\pi}}C*_{\pi_{2}}\overline{\tr}_{V^{\pi_{2}}}\check{R}_{2}

(50)

Proof.

From the
RCC
equation (
38
), let
\phi=C
, we have that

C^{(23)}\check{R}^{(12)}_{1}(z)=(\phi^{-1}\otimes\id)\check{R}^{(23)}_{2}(z)C^{(12)}(\id\otimes\phi)

then we have that

\begin{split}&\tr_{V^{\pi_{1}}\otimes V^{\pi}}C^{(23)}\check{R}^{(12)}_{1}(z)=\tr_{V^{\pi_{1}}\otimes V^{\pi}}(\phi^{-1}\otimes\id)\check{R}^{(23)}_{2}(z)C^{(12)}(\id\otimes\phi)\\
&=\tr_{V^{\pi}\otimes V^{\pi_{2}}}\check{R}^{(23)}_{2}(z)C^{(12)}\\
\end{split}

(51)

Then for any
\sum_{\alpha^{\prime},\beta^{\prime}}\alpha^{\prime}*_{\pi_{1}}\beta^{\prime}=\beta=\sum_{\beta^{\prime\prime},\gamma^{\prime}}\beta^{\prime\prime}*_{\pi_{2}}\gamma^{\prime}
, we take the component
\beta
of (
51
) and decompose the trace
\tr_{V^{\pi}\otimes V^{\pi_{1}}}
to
\tr_{V^{\pi}}
and
\tr_{V^{\pi_{1}}}
, similarly for the
\tr_{V^{\pi_{2}}\otimes V^{\pi}}
. We get the relation

\sum_{\alpha^{\prime},\beta^{\prime}}\overline{\tr}_{V^{\pi}_{\beta^{\prime}}}C^{(23)}\overline{\tr}_{V^{\pi_{1}}_{\alpha^{\prime}}}\check{R}^{(12)}_{1}(z)=\sum_{\beta^{\prime\prime},\gamma^{\prime}}\overline{\tr}_{V^{\pi_{2}}_{\gamma^{\prime}}}\check{R}^{(23)}_{2}(z)\overline{\tr}_{V^{\pi}_{\beta^{\prime\prime}}}C^{(12)}.

(52)

this is essentially the relation (
50
). ∎

3.3.
Weight zero relation and compatibility

From the RCC relation (
38
) and assume that
C
is right invertible (resp. left invertible and invertible), we multiply by the map
(C^{(12)}C^{(23)})_{r}^{-1}=(C^{(12)})^{-1}_{r}(C^{(23)})_{r}^{-1}
, we get the equation (
53
) in the space
\Hom(V^{\pi}\otimes_{*_{\pi_{2}}}V^{\pi_{2}}\otimes_{\pi_{2}}V^{\pi_{2}},V^{\pi}\otimes_{*_{\pi_{2}}}V^{\pi_{2}}\otimes_{\pi_{2}}V^{\pi_{2}})
.

C^{(12)}C^{(23)}\check{R}^{(12)}_{1}(z)\big(C^{(12)}C^{(23)}\big)_{r}^{-1}=\check{R}^{(23)}_{2}(z)

(53)

and graphically it is the following (
54
) which was observed by Roche as the equation
S^{\prime}

]

(54)

Then by taking the trace on
V^{\pi}_{\beta_{1}}
, for the
\beta_{1}
that connect
\gamma_{2}\circ\gamma_{1}
, we get the identity

\tr_{V^{\pi}}\big(C^{(12)}C^{(23)}\check{R}^{(12)}_{1}(z)\big(C^{(12)}C^{(23)}\big)_{r}^{-1}\big)=\tr_{V^{\pi}}\big(\check{R}^{(23)}_{2}(z)\big),

(55)

these kind of identities are graded by
\beta_{1}
or two copies of
\beta_{1}
.

But
\check{R}_{2}(z)
does not depend on
\beta_{1}
, the equality (
55
) will work for any
\beta\in t_{\pi}^{-1}(t_{\pi}(\beta_{1})
, if we assume that
\dim V^{\pi}_{\beta}=n
for all
\beta\in t_{\pi}^{-1}\big(t_{\pi}(\beta_{1})\big)
, then we will have that the LHS of
(
55
)
are all the same for all
\beta\in t_{\pi}^{-1}\big(t_{\pi}(\beta_{1})\big)
. This is the compatibility conditions for the target fibers of
\pi
.

Remark 3.13
.

We call (
54
) weight 0 or ice rule relation, because with the
\delta_{\beta_{1},\beta_{2}}
,
\gamma_{2}\circ\gamma_{1}
and
\gamma_{4}\circ\gamma_{3}
have the same start point and end point.

3.4.
Transposed intertwiner, groupoid structure and equivalence relation

In the previous subsections, we have discussed about the set
\pi
with the map
\operatorname{Ob}(\pi_{1})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle s_{\pi}}}{{\leftarrow}}\pi\stackrel{{\scriptstyle t_{\pi}}}{{\rightarrow}}\operatorname{Ob}(\pi_{2})
, the set can be seen as arrows pointing from
\pi_{1}
to
\pi_{2}
, now we consider the inverse direction.

Suppose that we have a set
\kappa
with the map
\operatorname{Ob}(\pi_{1})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle t_{\kappa}}}{{\leftarrow}}\kappa\stackrel{{\scriptstyle s_{\kappa}}}{{\rightarrow}}\operatorname{Ob}(\pi_{2})
, with the source and target direction defined, we can view
\kappa
as arrows from
\pi_{2}
to
\pi_{1}
, we use the letters
\rho
with subscripts to denote these kinds of arrows.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rm{Ob}(\pi_{1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rm{Ob}(\pi_{2})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa}

Example 3.14
.

For the set
\operatorname{Ob}(\pi_{1})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle s_{\pi}}}{{\leftarrow}}\pi\stackrel{{\scriptstyle t_{\pi}}}{{\rightarrow}}\operatorname{Ob}(\pi_{2})
, we can simply reverse the direction of arrows and then we get another set
\pi^{T}
,
(\pi,\pi^{T})
together give us a groupoid.

The fiber product and modules structures are defined analog to (
22
),(
23
) and (
24
). For the modules of convolution algebra, we can now consider the ”transpose” of previous example.

Example 3.15
.

For the vector space
\underline{\End}(V^{\pi_{1}})
defined in example
3.2
, we can use the following
T
presentation of each component

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(T)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V^{\pi_{1}}_{\alpha^{-1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha^{-1}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V^{\pi_{1}}_{(\alpha^{\prime})^{-1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha^{-1}_{1}}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rm{direction}}

(56)

and similarly for the space
\overline{\End}(V^{\pi_{1}})
, its component can be described by the following

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(T)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha^{-1}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V^{\pi_{1}}_{\alpha^{-1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha^{-1}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V^{\pi_{1}}_{(\alpha^{\prime})^{-1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rm{direction}}

(57)

Example 3.16
.

For
V^{\pi_{1}}\in\text{Vect}_{k}(\pi_{1})
and
V^{\pi_{2}}\in\text{Vect}_{k}(\pi_{2})
, let
\overline{\Hom}^{T}(V^{\pi_{2}},V^{\pi_{1}})
denote the
\kappa
graded vector space with component
\overline{\Hom}^{T}(V^{\pi_{2}},V^{\pi_{1}})_{\rho}=\oplus_{\alpha,\gamma}
with
\alpha\in\pi_{1}(t_{\kappa}(\rho),t_{\kappa}(\rho))
and
\gamma\in\pi_{2}(s_{\kappa}(\rho),s_{\kappa}(\rho))
, graphically it is described by

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(T)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V^{\pi}_{\alpha}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V^{\pi_{2}}_{\gamma}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rm{direction}}

(58)

Example 3.17
.

Let
\overline{\Hom}^{T}(V^{\pi_{1}},V^{\pi_{2}})
denote the
\kappa
graded vector space
\overline{\Hom}^{T}(V^{\pi_{1}},V^{\pi_{2}})_{\rho}=\oplus_{\alpha,\gamma}(V^{\pi_{1}}_{\alpha},V^{\pi_{2}}_{\gamma})
with
\alpha\in\pi_{1}(t_{\kappa}(\rho),t_{\kappa}(\rho))
and
\beta\in\pi_{2}(s_{\kappa}(\rho),s_{\kappa}(\rho))
, graphically it is described by

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(T)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V^{\pi_{2}}_{\gamma}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V^{\pi_{1}}_{\alpha}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rm{direction}}

(59)

The homomorphism
D\in\Hom(V^{\pi_{2}}\otimes_{*_{\pi_{2}}}V^{\kappa},V^{\kappa}\otimes_{*_{\pi_{1}}}V^{\pi_{1}})
is called
left invertible
if there exists a map
D^{-1}_{l}\in\Hom(V^{\kappa}\otimes_{*_{\pi_{1}}}V^{\pi_{1}},V^{\pi_{2}}\otimes_{*_{\pi_{2}}}V^{\kappa})
such that

D^{-1}_{l}D=\id_{V^{\pi_{2}}\otimes V^{\kappa}}

(60)

We can also define the
right invertibility
and
invertibility
similar to (
44
) and (
46
) by just interchanging the role of
\pi_{1}
and
\pi_{2}
.

Example 3.18
.

For a homomorphism
C\in\Hom(V^{\pi_{1}}\otimes_{*\pi_{1}}V^{\pi},V^{\pi}\otimes_{*_{\pi_{2}}}V^{\pi_{2}})
, let each component of
V^{\pi}
,
V^{\pi_{2}}
,
V^{\pi_{1}}
be one dimension. We further assume that
V^{\pi_{1}}_{\alpha}\simeq V^{\pi_{1}}_{\alpha^{-1}},V^{\pi_{2}}_{\gamma}\simeq V^{\pi_{2}}_{\gamma^{-1}}
and set
V^{\pi^{T}}_{\rho}=V^{\pi}_{\rho^{-1}}
, we can define the homomorphism
C^{T}\in\Hom(V^{\pi_{2}}\otimes_{*_{\pi_{2}}}V^{\pi^{T}},V^{\pi^{T}}\otimes_{*_{\pi_{1}}}V^{\pi_{1}})
as in (
61
) by specifying the coefficients of the homomorphism with respect to these one dimension basis.

(61)

Lemma 3.19
.

We have the following relation

\check{R}^{T}_{1}(z)^{(23)}\big(C^{T}\big)^{(12)}\big(C^{T}\big)^{(23)}=\big(C^{T}\big)^{(12)}\big(C^{T}\big)^{(23)}\check{R}^{T}_{2}(z)^{(12)}

(62)

Proof.

Inverse the direction of the equation
38
.
∎

Lemma 3.20
.

Suppose that
C
is left invertible with
C^{-1}_{l}
, then
C^{T}
is right invertible with
\big(C^{T}\big)_{r}=(C^{-1}_{l})^{T}
.

C^{T}(C^{-1}_{l})^{T}=\id_{V^{\pi^{T}}\otimes_{*_{\pi_{1}}}V^{\pi_{1}}}

(63)

and similar for the right invertibility and invertibility.

Definition 3.21
.

Suppose that
\check{R}_{1}(z)\in\operatorname{End}(V^{\pi_{1}}\otimes_{\pi_{1}}V^{\pi_{1}})
and
\check{R}_{2}(z)\in\operatorname{End}(V^{\pi_{2}}\otimes_{\pi_{2}}V^{\pi_{2}})
are two Yang–Baxter operators on the space of
V^{\pi_{1}}
and
V^{\pi_{2}}
, a homomorphism
D\in\Hom(V^{\pi_{2}}\otimes_{*_{\pi_{2}}}V^{\kappa},V^{\kappa}\otimes_{*_{\pi_{1}}}V^{\pi_{1}})
is called an transposed
(\check{R_{1}},\check{R_{2}})
intertwiner if it satisfies the following
RDD
relation

\check{R}_{1}(z)^{(23)}D^{(12)}D^{(23)}=D^{(12)}D^{(23)}\check{R}_{2}(z)^{(12)}

(64)

in the space
\Hom(V^{\pi_{2}}\otimes V^{\pi_{2}}\otimes V^{\kappa},V^{\kappa}\otimes V^{\pi_{1}}\otimes V^{\pi_{1}})
.

Proposition 3.22
.

Suppose that
D
is an
(\check{R}_{1},\check{R}_{2})
intertwiner and is invertible, then we have the following relation in the space
\Gamma(\kappa,\overline{\Hom}(V^{\pi_{2}},V^{\pi_{1}}))

\overline{\tr}_{V^{\kappa}}D*_{\pi_{1}}\overline{\tr}_{V^{\pi_{1}}}\check{R}_{1}(z)=\overline{tr}_{V^{\pi_{2}}}\check{R}_{2}(z)*_{\pi_{2}}\overline{\tr}_{V^{\kappa}}D

(65)

Suppose that
\check{R}^{T}_{1}(z)=\check{R}_{1}(z)
and also
\check{R}^{T}_{2}(z)=\check{R}_{2}(z)
, then compare the relation (
62
) and (
65
), we know that
C^{T}
is a transposed
(\check{R}_{1},\check{R}_{2})
intertwiner. We call an Yang–Baxter operator symmetric if

\check{R}^{T}(z)=\check{R}(z)

(66)

and from the inversion relation of
\check{R}
matrix (
17
), we know that
\check{R}^{T}(z)=\check{R}^{-1}(-z)

From the composition proposition
3.9
and the inversion relation
3.19
lemma for symmetric
\check{R}
matrix, then we have the following equivalence relation.

Proposition 3.23

(Symmetric equivalence)
.

The existence of intertwiner is an equivenlce relation among symmetric
\check{R}
matrices.

Remark 3.24
.

We can also define the complex conjugate transpose similar to (
61
), then also define similar notion as (
66
), which also forms th equivalence relation as in (
3.23
).

And also from proposition
3.12
and proposition
3.22
, we have

Proposition 3.25

(Transfer matrix equivalence)
.

The existence of an invertible intertwiner is an equivalence relation between symmetric
\check{R}
matrix and also the equation (
50
) holds among equivalence classes.

3.5.
Intertwiner with spectral parameters

More generally, we can define the following intertwiner with spectral parameter.

Definition 3.26
.

Suppose that
\check{R}_{1}(z)\in\operatorname{End}(V^{\pi_{1}}\otimes_{\pi_{1}}V^{\pi_{1}})
and
\check{R}_{2}(z)\in\operatorname{End}(V^{\pi_{2}}\otimes_{\pi_{2}}V^{\pi_{2}})
are two Yang–Baxter operators on the space of
V^{\pi_{1}}
and
V^{\pi_{2}}
, a map
C(z)\in\operatorname{Hom}(V^{\pi_{1}}\otimes_{*\pi_{1}}V^{\pi},V^{\pi}\otimes_{*\pi_{2}}V^{\pi_{2}})
is called an
(\check{R_{1}},\check{R_{2}})
intertwiner if it satisfies the following RCC relation

C(w)^{(12)}C(z)^{(23)}\check{R}_{1}(z-w)^{(12)}=\check{R}^{(23)}_{2}(z-w)C^{(12)}(z)C^{(23)}(w)

(67)

in the space
\operatorname{Hom}(V^{\pi_{1}}\otimes_{\pi_{1}}V^{\pi_{1}}\otimes_{*\pi_{1}}V^{\pi},V^{\pi}\otimes_{*\pi_{2}}V^{\pi_{2}}\otimes_{\pi_{2}}V^{\pi_{2}})
.

Proposition 3.27
.

Suppose that
C(z)
is an
(\check{R_{1}},\check{R_{2}})
intertwiner and there exists
C^{-1}(z)
satisfies the relation

C(z)C^{-1}(z)=\operatorname{id}_{V^{\pi}\otimes V^{\pi_{2}}}\in\operatorname{Hom}(V^{\pi}\otimes_{*\pi_{2}}V^{\pi_{2}},V^{\pi}\otimes_{*\pi_{2}}V^{\pi_{2}})

(68a)

C^{-1}(z)C(z)=\operatorname{id}_{V^{\pi_{1}}\otimes V^{\pi}}\in\operatorname{Hom}(V^{\pi_{1}}\otimes_{*\pi_{1}}V^{\pi},V^{\pi_{1}}\otimes_{*\pi_{1}}V^{\pi})

(68b)

then we have the following relation (
50
) in the space
\Gamma(\pi,\overline{\Hom}(V^{\pi_{1}},V^{\pi_{2}}))

\overline{\operatorname{tr}}_{V^{\pi_{1}}}\check{R}_{1}(z)*_{\pi_{1}}\overline{\operatorname{tr}}_{V^{\pi}}C(z^{\prime})=\overline{\operatorname{tr}}_{V^{\pi}}C(z^{\prime})*_{\pi_{2}}\overline{\operatorname{tr}}_{V^{\pi_{2}}}\check{R}_{2}(z)

(69)

.

Corollary 3.28

(
[
26
]
)
.

Suppose that we have dynamical
\check{R}_{1}
matrix on the vector space
V^{\pi_{1}}
, then set
\pi=\pi_{2}=\pi_{1}
and also
V^{\pi}=V^{\pi_{2}}=V^{\pi_{1}}
, then we can take the intertwiner
C:=\check{R}_{1}
, the RCC relation (
38
) will become the Yang-Baxter equation, then we get the commuting of the ”transfer matrix”,

\displaystyle\overline{\tr}_{V^{\pi_{1}}}\check{R}(z)*\overline{\tr}_{V^{\pi_{1}}}\check{R}(z^{\prime})=\overline{\tr}_{V^{\pi_{1}}}\check{R}(z^{\prime})*\overline{\tr}_{V^{\pi_{1}}}\check{R}(z)

(70)

The constructions in the previous sections are similar defined for the intertwiner with spectral parameters, just to be careful for the difference relation of the spectral parameters.

3.6.
Example: eight vertex-SOS intertwiner

The eight vertex-SOS intertwiner was discovered by Baxter in
[
6
]
and we use the parametrizations in the section 2.3 of
[
10
]
.

Let
H(z)
and
\Theta(z)
denote the Jacobian elliptic theta funcations with the half periods
K
and
iK^{\prime}
, they depends on the elliptic nome
p=e^{-\pi K^{\prime}/K}
as well as the parameter
z
.

H(z)=2\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}(-1)^{n-1}p^{(n-1/2)^{2}}\sin[(2n-1)\pi z/(2K)]

(71a)

\Theta(z)=1+2\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}(-1)^{n}p^{n^{2}}\cos(nz\pi/K)

(71b)

We define the function
\phi(p)=\prod_{k=1}^{\infty}(1-p^{k})
,
and let
h(z):=\zeta H(\lambda(z))\Theta(\lambda z)
where
\zeta=p^{-1/8}\phi(p)/\phi(p^{2})^{2}
,
\lambda
is a free parameter.

Let
\pi_{1}
denote the groupoid with a single vertex
v
and 2 edges
+
and
-
and set
V^{\pi_{1}}
be the groupoid graded vector space with each component one dimension,
V^{\pi_{1}}_{+}=\C v_{+}
and
V^{\pi_{1}}_{-}=\C v_{-}
.

(72)

The source fiber of
V^{\pi_{1}}\otimes_{\pi_{1}}V^{\pi_{1}}
is the space
\oplus_{\alpha}\in s^{-1}(a)(V^{\pi_{1}}\otimes V^{\pi_{1}})_{\alpha}
and it is isomorphic to the four dimensional vector space
\C^{2}\otimes\C^{2}
under the identification

v_{+}\to\begin{pmatrix}1\\
0\end{pmatrix},\quad v_{-}\to\begin{pmatrix}0\\
1\end{pmatrix}.

(73)

In this case, the Yang-Baxter operator
\check{R}_{1}
restricted to the source fiber can be written as a 4
\times
4 matrix. In other words the dynamical Yang–Baxter operator can be written as

\begin{split}\check{R}^{8v}(z)=\frac{\Theta(\lambda)\Theta(\lambda z)H(\lambda(z+1))}{\Theta(0)H(\lambda)\Theta(\lambda)}\begin{pmatrix}1&&&\\
&\frac{H(\lambda)H(\lambda z)}{\Theta(\lambda)\Theta(\lambda z)}&\frac{H(\lambda z)\Theta\big(\lambda(z+1)\big)}{\Theta(\lambda z)H\big(\lambda(z+1)\big)}&\\
&\frac{H(\lambda z)\Theta\big(\lambda(z+1)\big)}{\Theta(\lambda z)H\big(\lambda(z+1)\big)}&\frac{H(\lambda u)\Theta\big(\lambda(z+1)\big)}{\Theta(\lambda z)H\big(\lambda(z+1)\big)}&\\
&&&1\\
\end{pmatrix}\end{split}

(74)

Lemma 3.29
.

\check{R}^{\text{8v}}
is symmetric as defined in (
66
).

Remark 3.30
.

This is also called zero field condition by Baxter.

Remark 3.31
.

The transposed intertwiner defined in
3.4
is not the same as transposition of matrix. In the case of
\check{R}^{\text{8v}}
, they coincide simply because of
\check{R}^{\text{8v}}
is special, in the sense that
\check{R}^{\text{8v}}_{11}=\check{R}^{\text{8v}}_{44}=1
.

Let
\pi_{2}
be the groupoid that has the vertices
\Z+\xi,\xi\in\C
and the arrows are
a\to a-1
and
a\to a+1
for any
a\in\Z+\xi,\xi\in\C
. And we choose
V^{\pi_{2}}
to be the groupoid graded vector space with each component 1 dimension, that is
V^{\pi_{2}}_{a,a-1}=\C v_{a,-}
and
V^{\pi_{2}}_{a,a+1}=\C v_{a,+}
. And graphically it is simply (
75
)

\pi_{2}:\hbox to276.95pt{\vbox to33.31pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 138.47226pt\lower-14.66725pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

(75)

The source fibers of
V^{\pi_{2}}\otimes_{\pi_{2}}V^{\pi_{2}}
is the space
\oplus_{\alpha\in s^{-1}(a)}(V^{\pi_{2}}\otimes V^{\pi_{2}})_{\alpha}
and it is isomorphic to the four dimensional vector space
\C^{2}\otimes\C^{2}
under the identification

v_{a,+}\to\begin{pmatrix}1\\
0\end{pmatrix},\quad v_{a,-}\to\begin{pmatrix}0\\
1\end{pmatrix}.

(76)

In this case, the Yang-Baxter operator
\check{R}^{sos}(z)
restricted to the source fiber can be written as a
4\times 4
matrix. In other words the dynamical Yang–Baxter operator
\check{R}^{sos}(z,a)
can be written as

\check{R}^{\text{sos}}(z,a)=\frac{h(1)}{h(z+1)}\begin{pmatrix}1&&&\\
&\frac{h(a-z)h(1)}{h(a)h(z+1)}&\frac{h(a+1)h(z)}{h(a)h(z+1)}&\\
&\frac{h(a-1)h(z)}{h(a)h(z+1)}&\frac{h(a+z)h(1)}{h(a)h(z+1)}&\\
&&&1\\
\end{pmatrix}

(77)

The connecting groupoid
\pi
are the arrows from the vertex
v
of
\pi_{1}
to the object
\Z+\xi
of
\pi_{2}
, the vector space
V^{\pi}
is 1 dimension for each component,
V^{\pi}_{\beta}=\C v_{\beta}
.

(81)

Let
V^{\pi_{3}}=V^{\pi_{2}}
and
V^{\hat{\pi}}_{\beta_{a}}=\C e_{a}
for any
a
. By identifying the source fibers of
V^{\pi_{3}}
as (
76
). The
\check{R}^{\text{sym-sos}}
can be written as the following.

\check{R}^{\text{sym-sos}}(z,a)=\frac{h(1)}{h(z+1)}\begin{pmatrix}1&&&\\
&\frac{h(a-z)h(1)}{h(a)h(z+1)}&\frac{\sqrt{h(a+1)h(a-1)}h(z)}{h(a)h(z+1)}&\\
&\frac{\sqrt{h(a-1)h(a+1)}h(z)}{h(a)h(z+1)}&\frac{h(a+z)h(1)}{h(a)h(z+1)}&\\
&&&1\\
\end{pmatrix}

The transposed intertwiner defined in
3.4
is not the same as transposition of matrix. In the case of
\check{R}^{\text{sym-sos}}

\check{R}^{\text{sym-sos}}
is special, in the sense that
\check{R}^{\text{sym-sos}}_{11}=\check{R}^{\text{sym-sos}}_{44}=1
.

4.
Twist

In the subsection
3.2
, when we take partial traces of homomorphisms
\Hom(V^{\pi_{1}}\otimes V^{\pi},V^{\pi}\otimes V^{\pi_{2}})
, it gives rise to modules of convolution algebras. More generally we can also take ”matrix element” of various homomorphisms, this will gives us the various transfer operators defined below. We are mainly interested in the matrix element of the
\Hom(V^{\pi_{1}}\otimes V^{\pi},V^{\pi}\otimes V^{\pi_{2}})
and
\Hom(V^{\pi_{1}}\otimes V^{\pi},V^{\pi_{1}}\otimes V^{\pi})
, so the corresponding transfer operator will mainly be two types, we call them transfer operator of shape
\square
and
\rhd
.

4.1.
Transfer operator

Definition 4.1
.

A transfer operator of shape
\square
from
V^{\pi_{1}}
to
V^{\pi_{2}}
are graded by two arrows of
V^{\pi}
which denotes the initial degree and final degree. We denote the set of transfer operators by
\Gamma(\pi\times\pi,\overline{\operatorname{Hom}}(V^{\pi_{1}},V^{\pi_{2}}))
,
it consists of maps
l:\pi\times\pi\to\overline{\operatorname{Hom}}(V^{\pi_{1}},V^{\pi_{2}})
such that

(1)

l(\beta_{1},\beta_{2})\in\overline{\operatorname{Hom}}(V^{\pi_{1}},V^{\pi_{2}})_{\beta_{1},\beta_{2}}

(2)

for every
\beta_{1},\beta_{2}
, the number of
\alpha,\gamma
are finite.

and graphically it is simply

l(\beta_{1},\beta_{2})\in\oplus_{\alpha,\gamma}\hbox to90.92pt{\vbox to49.51pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 45.22092pt\lower-22.12502pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

(84)

The transfer operators have the following fusion product, for any
f,g\in\Gamma(\pi\times\pi,\overline{\operatorname{Hom}}(V^{\pi_{1}},V^{\pi_{2}}))
,
f*_{\pi_{1}}g\in\Gamma\big(\pi\times\pi,\overline{\operatorname{Hom}}(V^{\pi_{1}}\otimes_{\pi_{1}}V^{\pi_{1}},V^{\pi_{2}}\otimes_{\pi_{2}}V^{\pi_{2}})\big)
is defined by

(f*_{\otimes}g)(\beta_{1},\beta_{2})=\sum_{\beta}f(\beta,\beta_{2})\otimes_{\pi_{1}}g(\beta_{1},\beta)\in\overline{\operatorname{Hom}}(V^{\pi_{1}}\otimes_{\pi_{1}}V^{\pi_{1}},V^{\pi_{2}}\otimes_{\pi_{2}}V^{\pi_{2}}))

(85)

(86)

The transfer operators also carry action of the group
\operatorname{End}(V^{\pi_{2}})
, for an operator
Q\in\operatorname{End}(V^{\pi_{2}})
, the action is defined by composition with the image of each component of
l\in\Gamma\big(\pi\times\pi,\operatorname{Hom}(V^{\pi_{1}},V^{\pi_{2}})\big)
,

Ql:=Ql(\beta_{1},\beta_{2})

Similarly we can define the transfer operator
\Gamma\big(\pi\times\pi,\overline{\operatorname{Hom}}(V^{\pi_{2}},V^{\pi_{1}})\big)
, it consists of maps
l:\pi\times\pi\to\overline{\operatorname{Hom}}(V^{\pi_{2}},V^{\pi_{1}})
which graphically is

l(\beta_{1},\beta_{2})\in\oplus_{\alpha,\gamma}\hbox to89.24pt{\vbox to54.29pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 44.8598pt\lower-26.79916pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

(87)

and the transfer operators have the following fusion product

\displaystyle(f*g)(\beta_{1},\beta_{2})=\sum_{\beta}f(\beta_{1},\beta)\otimes_{\pi_{2}}g(\beta,\beta_{2})\in\overline{\operatorname{Hom}}(V^{\pi_{2}}\otimes_{\pi_{2}}V^{\pi_{2}},V^{\pi_{1}}\otimes_{\pi_{1}}V^{\pi_{1}})

and notice the difference of orders with (

) there is also the action of
\End(V^{\pi_{1}})
.

Example 4.2

(Verma type graded vector space)
.

A large source of examples of the transfer operator of type
\square
come from taking the matrix elements of homomorphism between groupoid vector space, one kind of these graded vector spaces that are most relevant to later considerations are the following.

A
\pi
graded vector space is of
Verma
type if for any
\beta\in\pi
, there is a one dimensional distinguished sub vector space generated by vector
v_{\beta}
for each
V^{\pi}_{\beta}
, then we can define the linear form

\displaystyle v^{*}_{\beta}(v)=1,\quad\rm{if}\quad v=v_{\beta}

\displaystyle v^{*}_{\beta}(v)=0,\quad\rm{if}\quad v\neq v_{\beta}

Assume that
V^{\pi}
is of
Verma type
, then for a map
J\in\operatorname{Hom}(V^{\pi_{1}}\otimes V^{\pi},V^{\pi}\otimes V^{\pi_{2}})
, we can define its matrix element
\operatorname{Mat}_{V^{\pi}}J\in\Gamma\big(\pi\times\pi,\overline{\operatorname{Hom}}(V^{\pi_{2}},V^{\pi_{1}})\big)
with respect to the distinguished basis element as follows

\operatorname{Mat}_{V^{\pi}}J:\beta_{1}\times\beta_{2}\mapsto\oplus_{\alpha,\gamma}(v^{*}_{\beta_{2}}\otimes 1)J(1\otimes v_{\beta_{1}})\in\overline{\operatorname{Hom}}(V^{\pi_{1}},V^{\pi_{2}})_{\beta_{1},\beta_{2}}

(89)

Now we introduce another type of graded vector space, let
V^{\pi_{1}}\in\operatorname{Vect}_{k}(\pi_{1})
, we use
\operatorname{End}_{\rhd}(V^{\pi_{1}})
to denote the collection of
\pi\times\pi
graded vector spaces, for each
\beta_{1},\beta_{2}
with
t_{\pi}(\beta_{1})=t_{\pi}(\beta_{2})
,
\operatorname{End}_{\rhd}(V^{\pi_{1}})_{\beta_{1},\beta_{2}}=\oplus_{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\beta_{1}\circ\alpha_{1}=\beta_{2}\circ\alpha_{2}}\Hom({V^{\pi_{1}}_{\alpha_{1}}},V^{\pi_{1}}_{\alpha_{2}})
, and
graphically it is simply

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}

(90)

Definition 4.3
.

Transfer operators of shape
\rhd
are maps from
\pi\times\pi\to\operatorname{End}_{\rhd}(V^{\pi_{1}})
with finite condition that for each
a\in\operatorname{\pi_{1}}
, there are finitely many arrows with source points
a
, we denote it by
\Psi(\pi\times\pi,\operatorname{End}_{\rhd}(V^{\pi_{1}}))
.

For any two maps
l_{1},l_{2}\in\Psi(\pi\times\pi,\operatorname{End}_{\rhd}(V^{\pi_{1}}))
, the product
l_{1}*_{\rhd}l_{2}\in\Psi(\pi\times\pi,\operatorname{End}_{\rhd}(V^{\pi_{1}}))
is defined by

l_{1}*_{\rhd}l_{2}(\beta_{1},\beta_{2})=\sum_{\beta}l_{1}(\beta,\beta_{2})l_{2}(\beta_{1},\beta)

(91)

And similarly we can define the space
\Psi(\pi\times\pi,\End_{\rhd}(V^{\pi_{2}}))
.

Example 4.4
.

When we consider the homomorphism
S\in\Hom(V^{\pi_{1}}\otimes V^{\pi},V^{\pi_{1}}\otimes V^{\pi})
, assume that
V^{\pi}
is of Verma type, then the matrix element
\rm{Mat}_{V^{\pi}}S\in\Psi(\pi\times\pi,\End(V^{\pi_{1}}))
of
S
with respect to the distinguished basis are

\rm{Mat}_{V^{\pi}}S:\beta_{1}\times\beta_{2}\mapsto\oplus_{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}}(v^{*}_{\beta_{2}}\otimes 1)S(v_{\beta_{1}}\otimes 1)\in\End_{\rhd}(V^{\pi_{1}})_{\beta_{1},\beta_{2}}

(92)

Definition 4.5

(Connecting system for
\pi_{2}
)
.

A connecting system for
\pi_{2}
is a set
\Sigma_{\pi_{2}}
of arrows in
\pi
, such that for each path
\gamma\in\pi_{2}
, there exists unique
\beta_{\gamma}\in\Sigma_{\pi_{2}},\beta_{\gamma}\in\pi
with the condition
t_{\pi}(\beta_{\gamma})=s_{\pi_{2}}(\gamma)
, they satisfy the compatible condition

\beta_{\gamma\circ\gamma^{\prime}}=\beta_{\gamma^{\prime}}.

(93)

And for each
\beta\in\Sigma_{\pi_{2}}
, there exist at least one
\gamma\in\pi_{2}
such that
\beta=\beta_{\gamma}
.

The connecting system is called
unique
if

s^{-1}_{\pi}(s_{\pi}(\beta_{\gamma}))=\beta_{\gamma},\forall\gamma

(94)

which means that there is only one unique edge starting from the point
s_{\pi}(\beta_{\gamma})
.

Definition 4.6

(Connecting system for
\pi_{1}
)
.

A connecting system for
\pi_{1}
is a set
\Sigma_{\pi_{1}}
of arrows in
\pi
, such that for each path
\alpha\in\pi_{1}
, there exists a unique
\beta^{\alpha}\in\pi
with
s_{\pi}(\beta)=t_{\pi_{1}}(\alpha)
and satisfies the compatible condition

\beta^{\alpha\circ\alpha^{\prime}}=\beta^{\alpha^{\prime}}.

(95)

And for each
\beta\in\Sigma_{\pi_{1}}
, there exist at least one
\alpha
such that
\beta=\beta^{\alpha}
.

The connecting system is called
unique
if
t_{\pi}^{-1}(t_{\pi}(\beta))=\beta
, that is there is only a unique edge with target the point
t_{\pi}(\beta)
.

Lemma 4.7
.

For each connecting system
\Sigma_{\pi_{1}}
, there exist an algebra map
\rhd
from
\End(V^{\pi_{1}})
to
\Psi(\pi\times\pi,\operatorname{End}_{\rhd}(V^{\pi_{1}}))
,

\rhd:\operatorname{End}(V^{\pi_{1}})\rightarrow\Psi(\pi\times\pi,\operatorname{End}_{\rhd}(V^{\pi_{1}})),l\mapsto l_{\rhd}

(96)

And also the inverse direction algebra map

\lhd:\Psi(\pi\times\pi,\operatorname{End}_{\rhd}(V^{\pi_{1}}))\rightarrow\operatorname{End}(V^{\pi_{1}}),f\mapsto f_{\lhd}

(97)

Proof.

For each operator
S\in\operatorname{End}(V^{\pi_{1}})
, with the choice of
\Sigma_{\pi_{1}}
, it induces a map
S_{\rhd}\in\Psi(\pi\times\pi,\operatorname{End}_{\rhd}(V^{\pi_{1}}))
, for each
\beta\in\Sigma_{\pi_{1}}
,

S_{\rhd}(\beta,\beta)=\oplus_{\alpha,t_{\pi_{1}}(\alpha)=s_{\pi}(\beta)}S_{\alpha}

(98)

other components of
S
are set to be 0.

And similarly, with the fixed choice of
\beta
, for each map
l\in\Psi(\pi\times\pi,\operatorname{End}(V^{\pi_{1}}))
, the component
l(\beta,\beta)
gives an operator in
\operatorname{End}(V^{\pi_{1}})
, this map is an algebra morphism.
∎

With the above preparation, we now can introduce the notion of invertibility for the transfer operator, which are parallel to the invertibility introduced in (
42
) and (
44
). The difference is that instead of the vector spaces graded
\pi
as in (
42
) and (
44
),
\pi
here are purely gradings and no vector spaces involved.

Definition 4.8
.

For
f\in\Gamma(\pi\times\pi,\overline{\operatorname{Hom}}(V^{\pi_{1}},V^{\pi_{2}}))
, it is
left invertible
if there exists
f^{-1}\in\Gamma(\pi\times\pi,\overline{\operatorname{Hom}}(V^{\pi_{2}},V^{\pi_{1}}))
such that

f^{-1}*_{\circ}f(\beta_{1},\beta_{2}):=\sum_{\beta}f^{-1}(\beta,\beta_{2})f(\beta_{1},\beta)=\delta_{\beta_{1},\beta_{2}}\operatorname{id}_{f}(\beta_{1},\beta_{1})\in\End_{\rhd}(V^{\pi_{1}})

(99)

here
\operatorname{id}_{f}\in\Psi(\pi\times\pi,\operatorname{End}_{\rhd}(V^{\pi_{1}}))
, and each of its component
\id_{f}(\beta_{1},\beta_{1})
is the identity map on
V^{\pi_{1}}_{\alpha}
for every
V^{\pi_{1}}_{\alpha}
that
f
transfers along
\beta_{1}
.

And it is
right invertible
if there exists
f^{-1}\in\Gamma(\pi\times\pi,\overline{\operatorname{Hom}}(V^{\pi_{2}},V^{\pi_{1}}))
such that

f*_{\circ}f^{-1}(\beta_{1},\beta_{2}):=\sum_{\beta}f^{-1}(\beta,\beta_{2})f(\beta_{1},\beta)=\delta_{\beta_{1},\beta_{2}}\operatorname{id}_{f^{-1}}(\beta_{1},\beta_{1})\in\End_{\rhd}(V^{\pi_{2}})

(100)

here
\id_{f^{-1}}\in\Psi\big(\pi\times\pi,\operatorname{End}_{\rhd}(V^{\pi_{2}})\big)
, and each of its component
\id_{f^{-1}}(\beta_{1},\beta_{1})
is identity map on
V^{\pi_{2}}_{\gamma}
for any
V^{\pi_{2}}_{\gamma}
that
f^{-1}
transfers along
\beta_{1}
.

f
is called invertible if it satisfies both (
99
) and (
100
) with the same
f^{-1}
.

Similar definition for the maps in
\Gamma(\pi\times\pi,\overline{\operatorname{Hom}}(V^{\pi_{2}},V^{\pi_{1}}))
and also its tensor product.

Lemma 4.9
.

Suppose that
f_{1},f_{2}\in\Gamma\big(\pi\times\pi,\overline{\operatorname{Hom}}(V^{\pi_{1}},V^{\pi_{2}})\big)
is right invertible with inverse maps
f_{1}^{-1},f_{2}^{-1}
, then
f_{1}*_{\otimes}f_{2}\in\Gamma(\pi\times\pi,\overline{\operatorname{Hom}}(V^{\pi_{1}}\otimes V^{\pi_{1}},V^{\pi_{2}}\otimes V^{\pi_{2}})
is right invertible with inverse
f^{-1}_{1}*_{\otimes}f^{-1}_{2}
.

Proof.

We can directly check the definitions

\displaystyle(f_{1}*_{\otimes}f_{2})*_{\circ}(f^{-1}_{1}*_{\otimes}f^{-1}_{2})(\beta_{1},\beta_{2})

(101)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\beta}(f_{1}*_{\otimes}f_{2})(\beta,\beta_{2})(f^{-1}_{1}*_{\otimes}f^{-1}_{2})(\beta_{1},\beta)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\beta^{\prime},\beta,\beta^{\prime\prime}}(f_{1}(\beta^{\prime},\beta_{2})\otimes_{\pi_{1}}f_{2}(\beta,\beta^{\prime})))(f^{-1}_{1}(\beta_{1},\beta^{\prime\prime})\otimes_{\pi_{2}}f^{-1}_{2}(\beta^{\prime\prime},\beta))

\displaystyle=\sum_{\beta^{\prime},\beta^{\prime\prime}}(f_{1}(\beta^{\prime},\beta_{2})f^{-1}_{1}(\beta_{1},\beta^{\prime\prime}))\otimes_{\pi_{2}}\sum_{\beta}f_{2}(\beta,\beta^{\prime})f^{-1}_{2}(\beta^{\prime\prime},\beta)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\beta^{\prime},\beta^{\prime\prime}}(f_{1}(\beta^{\prime},\beta_{2})f^{-1}_{1}(\beta_{1},\beta^{\prime\prime}))\otimes_{\pi_{2}}\delta_{\beta^{\prime},\beta^{\prime\prime}}\operatorname{id}_{f^{-1}_{2}}(\beta^{\prime},\beta^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\delta_{\beta_{1},\beta_{2}}\operatorname{id}_{f^{-1}_{1}\otimes f^{-1}_{2}}(\beta_{1},\beta_{1})

And in a similar way, we can prove the situation for the left inverse
(f^{-1}_{1}*_{\otimes}f^{-1}_{2})*_{\circ}(f_{1}*_{\otimes}f_{2})
and also the inverse.
∎

4.2.
Unique connecting system

We fix a unique connecting system
\Sigma_{\pi_{2}}
for
\pi_{2}
. Suppose that we have an Yang–Baxter operator
\check{R}_{1}
on
V^{\pi_{1}}
, given a right invertible map
j\in\Gamma(\pi\times\pi,\overline{\operatorname{Hom}}(V^{\pi_{2}}\otimes V^{\pi_{2}},V^{\pi_{1}}\otimes V^{\pi_{1}}))
, we can define the operator
\check{R}_{2}\in\Psi\big(\pi\times\pi,\operatorname{End}_{\rhd}(V^{\pi_{2}}\otimes V^{\pi_{2}})\big)
, for any
\beta_{1}\in\Sigma_{\pi_{2}}
,

\check{R}_{2}(\beta_{1},\beta_{1})=j^{-1}*_{\circ}(\check{R_{1}}j):=\sum_{\beta}j^{-1}(\beta,\beta_{1})\check{R}_{1}j(\beta_{1},\beta)

(102)

and graphically it is simply

\sum_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rm{red}}\hbox to235.86pt{\vbox to87.47pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 117.92693pt\lower-43.92615pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

(103)

By the lemma
4.7
above , the map
\check{R}_{2}
induce a map
(\check{R}_{2})_{\lhd}\in\operatorname{End}(V^{\pi_{2}}\otimes V^{\pi_{2}})
.

The map
j
is called a twist if there exists a right invertible map
q\in\Gamma(\pi\times\pi,\overline{\operatorname{Hom}}(V^{\pi_{2}}\otimes V^{\pi_{2}}\otimes V^{\pi_{2}},V^{\pi_{1}}\otimes V^{\pi_{1}}\otimes V^{\pi_{1}}))
such that

\operatorname{id}_{V^{\pi_{2}}}\otimes(\check{R}_{2})_{\lhd}=(q^{-1}*_{\circ}\big((\operatorname{id}_{V^{\pi_{1}}}\otimes\check{R}_{1})q\big))_{\lhd}

(104a)

(\check{R}_{2})_{\lhd}\otimes\operatorname{id}_{V^{\pi_{2}}}=(q^{-1}*_{\circ}\big((\check{R}_{1}\otimes\operatorname{id}_{V^{\pi_{1}}})q\big))_{\lhd}

(104b)

and graphically (
104b
) is just

(106)

Proposition 4.10
.

Suppose that the connecting system for
\pi_{2}
is unique, if
j
is a twist, then
(\check{R}_{2})_{\lhd}
is an Yang-Baxter operator on
V^{\pi_{2}}
.

Proof.

For each
\gamma\in\pi_{2}
, because the connecting system is unique, there exists a unique path
\beta_{1}
for
\gamma
with
t(\beta_{1})=s(\gamma)
and
s^{-1}_{\pi}(s_{\pi}(\beta_{1}))=\beta_{1}
.

\displaystyle\operatorname{id}_{V^{\pi_{2}}}\otimes(\check{R}_{2})_{\lhd}|_{(V^{\pi_{2}}\otimes V^{\pi_{2}}\otimes V^{\pi_{2}})_{\gamma}}

\displaystyle=\big(q^{-1}*_{\circ}\big((\operatorname{id}_{V^{\pi_{1}}}\otimes\check{R}_{1})q\big)(\beta_{1},\beta_{1})=\sum_{\beta}q^{-1}(\beta,\beta_{1})(\operatorname{id}_{V^{\pi_{1}}}\otimes\check{R}_{1})q(\beta_{1},\beta)

\displaystyle(\check{R}_{2})_{\lhd}\otimes\operatorname{id}_{V^{\pi_{2}}}|_{(V^{\pi_{2}}\otimes V^{\pi_{2}}\otimes V^{\pi_{2}})_{\gamma}}

\displaystyle=\big(q^{-1}*_{\circ}\big((\check{R}_{1}\otimes\operatorname{id}_{V^{\pi_{1}}})q\big)(\beta_{1},\beta_{1})=\sum_{\beta}q^{-1}(\beta,\beta_{1})(\check{R}_{1}\otimes\operatorname{id}_{V^{\pi_{1}}})q(\beta_{1},\beta)

then we have that

\displaystyle\big((\check{R}_{2})_{\lhd}\otimes\operatorname{id}_{V^{\pi_{2}}}\big)\big(\operatorname{id}_{V^{\pi_{2}}}\otimes(\check{R}_{2})_{\lhd}\big)|_{(V^{\pi_{2}}\otimes V^{\pi_{2}}\otimes V^{\pi_{2}})_{\gamma}}

\displaystyle=\big(\sum_{\beta^{\prime}}q^{-1}(\beta^{\prime},\beta_{1})(\check{R}_{1}\otimes\operatorname{id})q(\beta_{1},\beta^{\prime})\big)\big(\sum_{\beta}q^{-1}(\beta,\beta_{1})(\operatorname{id}\otimes\check{R}_{1})q(\beta_{1},\beta)\big)

By the right invertibility of the
q
and uniqueness of the connecting system, we have that

q(\beta_{1},\beta^{\prime})q^{-1}(\beta,\beta_{1})=\delta_{\beta,\beta^{\prime}}q*_{\circ}q^{-1}(\beta,\beta_{1})=\delta_{\beta,\beta^{\prime}}\id_{q^{-1}}(\beta,\beta)

(107)

so we have that

\displaystyle\big((\check{R}_{2})_{\lhd}\otimes\operatorname{id}_{V^{\pi_{2}}}\big)\big(\operatorname{id}_{V^{\pi_{2}}}\otimes(\check{R}_{2})_{\lhd}\big)|_{(V^{\pi_{2}}\otimes V^{\pi_{2}}\otimes V^{\pi_{2}})_{\gamma}}

\displaystyle=\big(\sum_{\beta}q^{-1}(\beta,\beta_{1})(\check{R}_{1}\otimes\operatorname{id})(\operatorname{id}\otimes\check{R}_{1})q(\beta_{1},\beta)\big)

then we know that it satisfies the Yang-Baxter equation.
∎

4.3.
Quasi-unique connecting system

In the last section, when we talk about twist, we assume that the connecting system is unique, this condition is too restrictive and eliminate the possibility of the symmetry.

Definition 4.11

(Quasi-unique connecting system)
.

Given a connecting system
\Sigma_{\pi_{2}}
,for each path
\gamma\in\pi_{2}
, we have a connecting path
\beta^{\prime}\in\Sigma_{\pi_{2}}
.
\Sigma_{\pi_{2}}
is called quasi-unique, if there exists a
\gamma^{\prime}\in\pi_{2}
with
t(\gamma^{\prime})=s(\gamma)
and there exist an edge
\beta_{1}\in\Sigma_{\pi_{2}}
with
t_{\pi}(\beta_{1})=s(\gamma^{\prime})
and the edge
\beta_{1}
satisfies the property (
108
), graphically it is (
109
).

s^{-1}_{\pi}(s_{\pi}(\beta_{1}))=\beta_{1}

(108)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s(\gamma^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s(\gamma)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t(\gamma)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta_{1}}
unique
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma}

(109)

Suppose we have an Yang-Baxter operator
\check{R}_{1}
on
V^{\pi_{1}}
, given an right invertible map
j\in\Gamma(\pi\times\pi,\overline{\operatorname{Hom}}(V^{\pi_{2}}\otimes V^{\pi_{2}},V^{\pi_{1}}\otimes V^{\pi_{1}}))
, we can define the operator
\check{R}_{2}\in\Psi(\pi\times\pi,\operatorname{End}_{\rhd}(V^{\pi_{2}}\otimes V^{\pi_{2}}))
, for any
\beta_{1},\beta_{2}\in\pi

\check{R}_{2}(\beta_{2},\beta_{1}):=j^{-1}*_{\circ}(\check{R}_{1}j)=\sum_{\beta}j^{-1}(\beta,\beta_{2})\check{R}_{1}j(\beta_{1},\beta)

(110)

graphically it is similar as (
103
).

The map
j
is called a twist for this quasi-unique connecting system, if for any length 3 path
\gamma
, there exists a right invertible map
q_{2}\in\Gamma(\pi\times\pi,\overline{\operatorname{Hom}}(V^{\pi_{2}}\otimes V^{\pi_{2}}\otimes V^{\pi_{2}},V^{\pi_{1}}\otimes V^{\pi_{1}}\otimes V^{\pi_{1}}))
, such that for any
\beta_{2}
with
t_{\pi}(\beta_{2})=s_{\pi_{2}}(\gamma)
and any
s_{\pi}(\beta^{\prime}_{2})=s_{\pi}(\beta_{2})
, we have the following weight zero or ice rule conditions (
111
).

\operatorname{id}_{V^{\pi_{2}}}\otimes(\check{R}_{2})_{\lhd}\delta_{\beta_{2},\beta^{\prime}_{2}}=\big(q_{2}^{-1}*_{\circ}\big((\operatorname{id}_{V^{\pi_{1}}}\otimes\check{R}_{1})q_{2}\big)\big)(\beta_{2},\beta^{\prime}_{2})

(111a)

(\check{R}_{2})_{\lhd}\otimes\operatorname{id}_{V^{\pi_{2}}}\delta_{\beta_{2},\beta^{\prime}_{2}}=\big(q_{2}^{-1}*_{\circ}\big((\check{R}_{1}\otimes\operatorname{id}_{V^{\pi_{1}}})q_{2}\big)\big)(\beta_{2},\beta^{\prime}_{2})

(111b)

And graphically the equation (
111b
) is equivalent to the following two relations

(112b)

q\in\Gamma(\pi\times\pi,\overline{\operatorname{Hom}}((V^{\pi_{2}})^{\otimes n},(V^{\pi_{1}})^{\otimes n}))
satisfies the following conditions

\operatorname{id}^{\otimes n-2}_{V^{\pi_{2}}}\otimes(\check{R}_{2})_{\lhd}|_{(V^{\pi_{2}})^{\otimes n}_{\gamma\gamma^{\prime}}}=(q^{-1}*_{\circ}\big((\operatorname{id}^{\otimes n-2}_{V^{\pi_{1}}}\otimes\check{R}_{1})q\big))_{\lhd}

(113a)

(113b)
