(114)

And there exists an invertible map
q_{1}\in\Gamma\big(\pi\times\pi,\overline{\operatorname{Hom}}((V^{\pi_{2}})^{\otimes n-3},(V^{\pi_{1}})^{\otimes n-3})\big)
such that the factorization property holds (
115
).

q|_{(V^{\pi_{2}})^{\otimes n}_{\gamma\gamma^{\prime}}}=q_{1}|_{(V^{\pi_{2}})^{\otimes n-3}_{\gamma^{\prime}}}*_{\otimes}q_{2}|_{(V^{\pi_{2}})^{\otimes 3}_{\gamma}}

(115)

Proposition 4.12
.

Suppose that the connecting system for
\pi_{2}
is quasi-unique and there is no multi-edge in
\pi
, if
j
is a generalized twist, then
(\check{R}_{2})_{\rhd}
is an Yang-Baxter operator on
V^{\pi_{2}}
.

Proof.

From the factorization condition (
115
) and the lemma
4.9
, we have the relation that

\displaystyle\big(q^{-1}*_{\circ}(\operatorname{id}^{\otimes n-2}_{V^{\pi_{1}}}\otimes\check{R}_{1})q\big)(\beta_{1},\beta_{1})

\displaystyle=(q^{-1}_{1}*_{\otimes}q^{-1}_{2})*_{\circ}\big((\operatorname{id}^{\otimes n-2}_{V^{\pi_{1}}}\otimes\check{R}_{1})q_{1}*_{\otimes}q_{2}\big)(\beta_{1},\beta_{1})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\beta}q^{-1}_{1}*_{\otimes}q^{-1}_{2}(\beta,\beta_{1})(\operatorname{id}^{\otimes n-2}_{V^{\pi_{1}}}\otimes\check{R}_{1})q_{1}*_{\otimes}q_{2}(\beta_{1},\beta)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\beta,\beta_{2},\beta^{\prime}_{2}}\big(q^{-1}_{1}(\beta^{\prime}_{2},\beta_{1})\otimes q^{-1}_{2}(\beta,\beta^{\prime}_{2})\big)(\operatorname{id}^{\otimes n-2}_{V^{\pi_{1}}}\otimes\check{R}_{1})(q_{1}(\beta_{1},\beta_{2})\otimes q_{2}(\beta_{2},\beta))

Then from the condition (
111b
) and the (
111a
), we know that

\big(q^{-1}*_{\circ}(\operatorname{id}^{\otimes n-2}_{V^{\pi_{1}}}\otimes\check{R}_{1})q\big)(\beta_{1},\beta_{1})=\id_{q_{1}}(\beta_{1},\beta_{1})\otimes\id_{V^{\pi_{2}}}\otimes(\check{R_{2}})_{\lhd}

(116)

and similarly we get the equation

q^{-1}*_{\circ}\big((\id^{\otimes n-3}_{V^{\pi_{1}}}\otimes\check{R}_{1}\otimes\operatorname{id}^{\otimes n-2}_{V^{\pi_{1}}})q\big)(\beta_{1},\beta_{1})=\id_{q_{1}}(\beta_{1},\beta_{1})\otimes(\check{R_{2}})_{\lhd}\otimes\id_{V^{\pi_{2}}}

(117)

Then we multiply these two operators

\displaystyle\big(q^{-1}*_{\circ}(\operatorname{id}^{\otimes n-2}_{V^{\pi_{1}}}\otimes\check{R}_{1})q\big)(\beta_{1},\beta_{1})\big(q^{-1}*_{\circ}\big((\id^{\otimes n-3}_{V^{\pi_{1}}}\otimes\check{R}_{1}\otimes\operatorname{id}^{\otimes n-2}_{V^{\pi_{1}}})q\big)(\beta_{1},\beta_{1})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\beta,\beta^{\prime}}\big(q^{-1}(\beta,\beta_{1})(\operatorname{id}^{\otimes n-2}_{V^{\pi_{1}}}\otimes\check{R}_{1})q(\beta_{1},\beta)\big)\big(q^{-1}(\beta^{\prime},\beta_{1})(\id^{\otimes n-3}_{V^{\pi_{1}}}\otimes\check{R}_{1}\otimes\operatorname{id}^{\otimes n-2}_{V^{\pi_{1}}})q(\beta_{1},\beta^{\prime})\big)

And then from the property (
108
) and the right invertibility of
q^{-1}_{1}
, we have that

q(\beta_{1},\beta)q^{-1}(\beta^{\prime},\beta_{1})=\delta_{\beta^{\prime},\beta}\id_{q^{-1}}

we get the relation

\begin{split}&\big(q^{-1}*_{\circ}(\operatorname{id}^{\otimes n-2}_{V^{\pi_{1}}}\otimes\check{R}_{1})q\big)(\beta_{1},\beta_{1})\big(q^{-1}*_{\circ}\big((\id^{\otimes n-3}_{V^{\pi_{1}}}\otimes\check{R}_{1}\otimes\operatorname{id}^{\otimes n-2}_{V^{\pi_{1}}})\big)q\big)(\beta_{1},\beta_{1})\\
&=\big(q^{-1}*_{\circ}\big(\operatorname{id}^{\otimes n-3}_{V^{\pi_{1}}}\otimes(\id_{V^{\pi_{1}}}\otimes\check{R}_{1})(\check{R}_{1}\otimes\operatorname{id}_{V^{\pi_{1}}})q\big)(\beta_{1},\beta_{1})\\
&=\id_{q_{1}}(\beta_{1},\beta_{1})\otimes(\id_{V^{\pi_{2}}}\otimes(\check{R_{2}})_{\lhd})((\check{R_{2}})_{\lhd}\otimes\id_{V^{\pi_{2}}})\end{split}

(118)

And then from the equation (
118
) and the fact that
\check{R_{1}}
is an Yang-Baxter operator, we know that
(\check{R}_{2})_{\lhd}
satisfies the Yang-Baxter equation

\begin{split}&(\id_{V^{\pi_{2}}}\otimes(\check{R_{2}})_{\lhd})((\check{R_{2}})_{\lhd}\otimes\id_{V^{\pi_{2}}})(\id_{V^{\pi_{2}}}\otimes(\check{R_{2}})_{\lhd})\\
&=((\check{R_{2}})_{\lhd}\otimes\id_{V^{\pi_{2}}})(\id_{V^{\pi_{2}}}\otimes(\check{R_{2}})_{\lhd})((\check{R_{2}})_{\lhd}\otimes\id_{V^{\pi_{2}}})\end{split}

(119)

∎

4.4.
Intertwiner from twists

Suppose now that we have a
quasi-unique connecting system
for
\pi_{2}
and an invertible
\widetilde{C}\in\Gamma\big(\pi\times\pi,\overline{\operatorname{Hom}}(V^{\pi_{1}},V^{\pi_{2}})\big)
with its inverse
\widetilde{C}^{-1}\in\Gamma\big(\pi\times\pi,\overline{\operatorname{Hom}}(V^{\pi_{2}},V^{\pi_{1}})\big)
.

Let
j=\widetilde{C}^{-1}*_{\otimes}\widetilde{C}^{-1}\in\Gamma(\pi\times\pi,\overline{\operatorname{Hom}}(V^{\pi_{2}}\otimes V^{\pi_{2}},V^{\pi_{1}}\otimes V^{\pi_{1}}))
, from the lemma
4.9
, it has inverse with
j^{-1}=\widetilde{C}*_{\otimes}\widetilde{C}
. With the map
j
and a given quasi-unique connecting system, we can define
\check{R}_{2}\in\Psi\big(\pi\times\pi,\operatorname{End}_{\rhd}(V^{\pi_{2}}\otimes V^{\pi_{2}})\big)
as before

\check{R}_{2}(\beta_{2},\beta_{1})=j^{-1}*_{\circ}(\check{R}_{1}j):=\sum_{\beta}j^{-1}(\beta,\beta_{2})\check{R}_{1}j(\beta_{1},\beta)

(120)

Definition 4.13
.

\widetilde{C}
is called a cell-twist if for any
\gamma\in\pi_{2}
, we have the following relations

\sum_{\beta}j^{-1}(\beta,\beta_{1})\check{R}_{1}j(\beta_{1},\beta)=\sum_{\beta^{\prime}}j^{-1}(\beta^{\prime},\beta^{\prime}_{1})\check{R}_{1}j(\beta^{\prime}_{1},\beta^{\prime})

(121a)

\sum_{\beta}j^{-1}(\beta,\beta_{2})\check{R}_{1}j(\beta_{1},\beta)=0

(121b)

for any
\beta_{1}
,
\beta^{\prime}_{1}
with
t_{\pi}(\beta^{\prime}_{1})=t_{\pi}(\beta_{1})=s_{\pi_{2}}(\gamma)
and for any
\beta_{2}\neq\beta_{1}
with
s_{\pi}(\beta_{2})=s_{\pi}(\beta_{1})
.

Proposition 4.14
.

Suppose that
\widetilde{C}
is a cell-twist, then
j
is a twist for the chosen quasi-unique connecting system.

Proof.

With the given quasi-connecting system, the corresponding
q_{1}
is
(n-3)
times
*_{\otimes}
of
\widetilde{C}^{-1}
and
q_{2}=\widetilde{C}^{-1}*_{\otimes}\widetilde{C}^{-1}*_{\otimes}\widetilde{C}^{-1}
as in the definition of quasi-unique twist and the relation (
111b
),(
111a
), (
113b
) and (
113a
) follows from (
121
) and the invertibility of
\widetilde{C}
.
∎

The simplest cell twist is the following gauge transform.

Definition 4.15

(Gauge transform)
.

For each (
\pi_{1},V^{\pi_{1}},\check{R}_{1})
, we can assume that
\pi_{1}=\pi_{2},V^{\pi_{2}}=V^{\pi_{2}}
and
\pi
is the arrows which connect the same vertices.

We now define the following map
\widetilde{C}^{-1}\in\Gamma(\pi\times\pi,\overline{\operatorname{Hom}}(V^{\pi_{2}},V^{\pi_{1}}))
, for each of the component (
122
),

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\circ_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\circ_{j}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet_{j}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V^{\pi_{2}}_{\alpha}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V^{\pi_{1}}_{\alpha}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}

(122)

let
c_{\alpha,\beta}
to be nonzero complex number, then
\widetilde{C}^{-1}
is defined to be

\begin{split}\widetilde{C}^{-1}(\beta,\beta):V^{\pi_{2}}_{\alpha}&\to V_{\alpha}^{\pi_{1}}:v\mapsto c_{\alpha,\beta}v\end{split}

(123)

\widetilde{C}^{-1}
is easily seen to be invertible with the inverse defined by the inverse of the complex numbers
c_{\alpha,\beta}
. And the condition (
134
) are easily verified, because in this case, there is only one unique connecting edge for each vertex. So
j=\widetilde{C}^{-1}*_{\otimes}\widetilde{C}^{-1}
is a twist, and the
(\check{R}_{2})_{\lhd}
defined by (
120
) is called the gauge transform of
\check{R}_{1}
.

Now we can formulate the relation with the intertwiner.

Proposition 4.16
.

For each
\beta\in\pi
, we can assign a one dimensional vector space, then it forms a vector space
V^{\pi}
, let
\widetilde{C}
be a cell-twist, then it gives rise to an intertwiner
C\in\operatorname{Hom}(V^{\pi_{1}}\otimes_{*\pi_{1}}V^{\pi},V^{\pi}\otimes_{*\pi_{2}}V^{\pi_{2}})
.

Proof.

In the case of one dimension for each edge
\beta
, the relation (
54
) and the relation (
121
) are the same. And in this case also the RCC relation (
38
) is equivalent to the relation (
53
).
∎

5.
Example: Drinfeld twist

We first recall the theory of twist for quasi-triangular Hopf algebra by Drinfeld in
[
12
,
13
,
14
]
, see also the section 8.10 of
[
15
]
. Let
(H,m,\Delta,\epsilon,S)
be a Hopf algebra, the iterated coproduct for
i\geq 1
is

\Delta_{i}:H^{\otimes n}\to H^{\otimes n+1},\quad\Delta_{i}=\operatorname{id}\otimes\dots\otimes\Delta\otimes\dots\operatorname{id}

with the convention
\Delta_{0}=1\otimes(-)
and
\Delta_{n+1}=(-)\otimes 1
, an
n
cochain
\chi
is an invertible element of
H^{\otimes n}
and we define its coboundary as the
n+1
cochain

\displaystyle\partial\chi:=(\prod_{i=0}^{i~\text{odd}}\Delta_{i}\chi)(\prod_{i=0}^{i~\text{even}}\Delta_{i}\chi^{-1})

the cochain
\chi
is an cocycle if
\partial\chi=1
,
An
n
cocycle for a Hopf algebra or bialgebra is an invertible element
\chi
in
H^{\otimes n}
such that
\partial\chi=1
, finally a cochain or cocycle is counital if
\epsilon_{i}\chi=1
.

Definition 5.1
.

Let
J\in H\otimes H
be an invertible element, then
J
is called a Drinfeld twist if it is a 2 cocycle

(\Delta\otimes\operatorname{id})(J)(J\otimes 1)=(\operatorname{id}\otimes\Delta)(J)(1\otimes J)

(124)

or equivalently
J^{12,3}J^{1,2}=J^{1,23}J^{2,3}
.

Proposition 5.2

(
[
15
]
,Exercise 5.14.2.)
.

Let
\Delta_{J}:H\to H\otimes H
be given by

\Delta_{J}(x):=J^{-1}\Delta(x)J

then there exist
S_{J}:H\to H
such that
(H,m,\Delta_{J},\epsilon,S_{J})
is a Hopf algebra and is called the twist of
H
by
J
, denoted by
H^{J}
.

Proposition 5.3

(
[
15
]
,Proposition 8.3.14.)
.

If
(H,R)
is a quasi-triangular Hopf algebra with twist
J
, then
(H^{J},R^{J})
, where
R_{J}=(J^{21})^{-1}RJ
is also quasi-triangular.

Now we reformulate the definition of Drinfeld twist and give a link with the twist developed in subsection
4.2
. We choose
V
to be a representation of the quasi-triangular Hopf algebra
(H,R)
, and
P:V\otimes V\to V\otimes V
the permutation matrix and
\check{R}:=PR
, suppose we have a twist, then we have

\displaystyle\check{R}_{J}

\displaystyle=P(J^{21})^{-1}RJ=P^{12}P^{12}(J^{12})^{-1}PR^{12}J^{12}

(125)

\displaystyle=(J^{12})^{-1}\check{R}^{(12)}J^{12}

\displaystyle\check{R}_{J}\otimes\operatorname{id}

\displaystyle=(J^{12})^{-1}\check{R}^{(12)}\Delta\otimes\operatorname{id}(J^{-1})\Delta\otimes\operatorname{id}(J)J^{12}

\displaystyle=(J^{12})^{-1}\Delta\otimes\operatorname{id}(J^{-1})\check{R}^{(12)}\Delta\otimes\operatorname{id}(J)J^{12}

\displaystyle=(J^{12,3}J^{12})^{-1}\check{R}^{(12)}J^{12,3}J^{12}

\displaystyle\operatorname{id}\otimes\check{R}_{J}

\displaystyle=(J^{1,23}J^{23})^{-1}\check{R}^{23}(J^{1,23}J^{23})

And notice that the cocycle equation appears in the two side of
\operatorname{id}\otimes\check{R}
and
\check{R}\otimes\operatorname{id}
, so we can have the following equivalent way of defining Drinfeld twist

Definition 5.4
.

[Drinfeld Twist] Suppose we have a vector space
V
and a Yang-Baxter operator
\check{R}
acting on
V\otimes V
, an invertible operator
J:V\otimes V\to V\otimes V
is a twist if there exist invertible operator
Q:V\otimes V\otimes V\to V\otimes V\otimes V
such that:

\displaystyle\check{R}_{J}=J^{-1}\check{R}J

(126)

\displaystyle\check{R}_{J}\otimes\operatorname{id}=Q(\check{R}\otimes\operatorname{id})Q^{-1}

\displaystyle\operatorname{id}\otimes\check{R}_{J}=Q(\operatorname{id}\otimes\check{R})Q^{-1}

In this case, we can assume that the groupoid is one point
a
with one edge
\alpha
as follows

\pi_{1}=\hbox to61.73pt{\vbox to38.09pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 30.86302pt\lower-5.8125pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

And

, the operators

in subsection

.

6.
Example: dynamical twist

We first recall the Etingof–Varchenko construction
[
21
]
of dynamical twist in terms of fusion operator for any semisimple lie algebra and its corresponding quantum group, following the book
[
16
]
.

Let
q
be a nonzero complex number which is not a root of unity and
\mathfrak{g}
be a semisimple Lie algebra. And
V
be a finite dimensional representations of the quantum group
U_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
,
R
denote the
R
matrix of the quantum group. For generic weight
\lambda\in\mathfrak{h}^{*}
, there exists universal fusion operator
J(\lambda)
and it has the following property.

Theorem 6.1

(
[
16
]
,Theorem 3.8)
.

The universal fusion operator
J(\lambda)
satisfies the dynamical twist equation on the space
V\otimes V\otimes V
.

J^{12,3}(\lambda)J^{1,2}(\lambda-h^{3})=J^{1,23}(\lambda)J^{2,3}(\lambda)

Here the superscripts of
J
stands for both the components on which the first and second components of
J
act and the coproduct action of quantum group, for example
J^{1,23}
means
(1\otimes\Delta)(J)
. The dynamical notation means

J^{1,2}(\lambda-h^{3})(a_{1}\otimes a_{2}\otimes a_{3})=J^{1,2}(\lambda-\rm{weight}(a_{3}))(a_{1}\otimes a_{2}\otimes a_{3})

Corollary 6.2

(
[
16
]
,Corallory 3.11)
.

The fusion operator
J(\lambda)
also satisfies

J^{32,1}(\lambda)J^{32}(\lambda-h^{1})=J^{3,21}(\lambda)J^{21}(\lambda)

Theorem 6.3

(
[
16
]
,Theorem 3.15)
.

The exchange operator
R_{J}(\lambda):V\otimes V\to V\otimes V
is defined by
R_{J}(\lambda)=J^{-1}(\lambda)R^{21}J^{21}(\lambda)
, it satisfies the dynamical Yang-Baxter equation

R_{J}^{12}(\lambda-h^{3})R_{J}^{13}(\lambda)R_{J}^{23}(\lambda-h^{1})=R^{23}_{J}(\lambda)R^{13}_{J}(\lambda-h^{2})R_{J}^{12}(\lambda)

We give another proof that the exchange operator
R_{J}(\lambda)
satisfies the dynamical Yang-Baxter equation, then reformulate the definition of twist, we first have the following lemma. The
R
matrix has another version with multiplication by a permutation matrix

\check{R}_{J}(\lambda)=PR_{J}(\lambda)=(J^{21}(\lambda))^{-1}PR^{21}J^{21}

Lemma 6.4
.

Let
\tilde{R}=RP
, then we have

\tilde{R}^{12}\tilde{R}^{23}\tilde{R}^{12}=\tilde{R}^{23}\tilde{R}^{12}\tilde{R}^{23}

(127)

Proof.

Because the left hand side of (
127
) equals
P^{13}R^{32}R^{31}R^{21}
and the right hand side of (
127
) equals
P^{13}R^{21}R^{31}R^{32}
and
R
satisfies the Yang-Baxter equation, so the equation (
127
) follows.
∎

We also have the relations,

\displaystyle\check{R}_{J}(\lambda)\otimes\operatorname{id}_{V}

\displaystyle=(J^{21}(\lambda))^{-1}P^{12}R^{21}J^{21}(\lambda)

(128)

\displaystyle=(J^{21}(\lambda))^{-1}P^{12}R^{21}(J^{3,21}(\lambda))^{-1}J^{3,21}(\lambda)J^{21}(\lambda)

\displaystyle=(J^{21}(\lambda))^{-1}P^{12}P^{21}(J^{3,21}(\lambda))^{-1}P^{21}R^{21}J^{3,21}(\lambda)J^{21}(\lambda)

\displaystyle=(J^{3,21}(\lambda)J^{21}(\lambda))^{-1}R_{12}P_{12}(J^{3,21}(\lambda)J^{21}(\lambda))

\displaystyle\operatorname{id}_{V}\otimes\check{R}_{J}(\lambda-h^{1})

\displaystyle=(J^{32}(\lambda-h^{1}))^{-1}P^{23}R^{32}J^{32}(\lambda-h^{1})

(129)

\displaystyle=(J^{32}(\lambda-h^{1}))^{-1}P^{23}R^{32}(J^{32,1}(\lambda))^{-1}J^{32,1}(\lambda)J^{32}(\lambda-h^{1})

\displaystyle=(J^{32}(\lambda-h^{1}))^{-1}P^{23}P^{32}(J^{32,1}(\lambda))^{-1}P^{32}R^{3}2J^{32,1}(\lambda)J^{32}(\lambda-h^{1})

\displaystyle=(J^{32,1}(\lambda)J^{32}(\lambda-h^{1}))R^{23}P^{23}(J^{32,1}(\lambda)J^{32}(\lambda-h^{1}))

From equation (
127
) and the corollary
6.2
, we know that
\check{R}(\lambda)
satisfies the equation:

\check{R}^{(23)}_{J}(\lambda-h^{1})\check{R}_{J}^{(12)}(\lambda)\check{R}_{J}^{(23)}(\lambda-h^{1})=\check{R}_{J}^{(12)}(\lambda)\check{R}_{J}^{(23)}(\lambda-h^{1})\check{R}_{J}^{(12)}(\lambda)

From the proof above, we can reformuate the dynamical drinfeld twist as the following.

Definition 6.5
.

Suppose we have a vector space
V
and a Yang-Baxter operator
\check{R}
acting on
V\otimes V
, an invertible operator
J(\lambda):V\otimes V\to V\otimes V
is a twist if there exists invertible operator
Q(\lambda):V\otimes V\otimes V\to V\otimes V\otimes V
such that

\displaystyle\check{R}_{J}(\lambda)=J^{-1}(\lambda)\check{R}J(\lambda)

(130)

\displaystyle\check{R}_{J}(\lambda)\otimes\operatorname{id}=Q(\lambda)(\check{R}\otimes\operatorname{id})Q^{-1}(\lambda)

\displaystyle\operatorname{id}\otimes\check{R}_{J}(\lambda-h^{1})=Q(\lambda)(\operatorname{id}\otimes\check{R})Q^{-1}(\lambda)

then it follows automatically,
\check{R}_{J}
satisfies the Yang-Baxter equation.

In order to be more explicitly show the relation with the groupoids twist in the subsection
4.2
.

6.1.
The
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
case preliminary

We consider the lie algebra
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
over
\C
, let
M_{\lambda}
denote the Verma module over
\mathfrak{g}
with highest weight
\lambda\in\mathfrak{g}^{*}
,
x_{\lambda}
being its highest weight vector and
x^{*}_{\lambda}
the lowest weight vector of the dual module. Let
V=\rm{span}\{v_{+},v_{-}\}
be the two dimensional representation of
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
, the intertwining operator
\Phi:M_{\lambda}\to M_{\mu}\otimes V
, the vector
x_{\mu}^{*}(\Phi x_{\lambda})\in V[\lambda-\mu]
is called the expectation value of
\Phi
and denoted by
\langle\Phi\rangle
.

Lemma 6.6
.

M_{\lambda}
is irreducible for
\lambda\neq 0,1,2,\dots
, i.e all positive integers and 0.

Lemma 6.7
.

If
M_{\mu}
is irreducible, then for all homogeneous
v\in V
, there exists a unique intertwining operator
\Phi:M_{\mu+\rm{wt}v}\to M_{\mu}\otimes V
such that
\langle\Phi\rangle=v
, this intertwine will be denoted by
\Phi^{v}_{\mu+\rm{wt}v}
.

Let
V,W
be finite-dimensional representations of
\mathfrak{g}
, fix a generic
\lambda
, let
\gamma,\beta\in\mathfrak{h}^{*}
and
w\in W[\gamma],v\in V[\beta]
, the assignment

\displaystyle w,v\to\langle(\Phi^{w}_{\lambda-\beta}\otimes\id)\Phi^{v}_{\lambda}\rangle\in(W\otimes V)[\gamma+\beta]

combining these maps for all
\gamma,\beta
, we get a linear map
J_{WV}(\lambda):W\otimes V\to W\otimes V

Theorem 6.8
.

These operator satisfy the following dynamical twist equation in
V\otimes W\otimes U
:

\displaystyle J^{12,3}_{V\otimes W,U}(\lambda)J^{(12)}_{VW}(\lambda-h^{3})=J^{1,23}_{V,W\otimes U}(\lambda)J^{23}_{WU}(\lambda)

Let
P_{12}:W\otimes V\to V\otimes W
denote the permutation operator and let
J^{21}_{VW}(\lambda):W\otimes V\to W\otimes V
be defined as follows:
J^{21}_{V}W:=P_{12}J_{VW}(\lambda)P_{12}
, we can define the following operators on
V\otimes W\otimes U:

\displaystyle J^{13}_{VU}(\lambda-h^{2}):=P_{23}J^{12}_{VU}(\lambda-h^{3})P_{23}

\displaystyle J^{23}_{WU}(\lambda-h^{1}):=P_{12}P_{23}J^{12}_{WU}(\lambda-h^{3})P_{23}P_{12}

\displaystyle J^{13,2}_{V\otimes U,W}(\lambda)=P_{23}J^{12,3}_{V\otimes U,W}P_{23}

other notations are defined similarly by using there permutation operators.

Corollary 6.9
.

\displaystyle J^{32}_{UW}(\lambda-h^{1})=J^{23,1}_{W\otimes U,V}(\lambda)^{-1}J^{3,12}_{U,V\otimes W}(\lambda)J^{21}_{WV}(\lambda)

we also know that there exists an obvious Yang-Baxter operator
R_{1}=P
on
V
, then we can define the following operator

\displaystyle R_{2}(\lambda)=(J_{\lambda})^{-1}R_{1}J_{\lambda}=(J_{VV}(\lambda)P)^{-1}P(J_{VV}(\lambda)P)

Then we have the following

\displaystyle R_{2}(\lambda)\otimes\id

\displaystyle=\left((J_{VV}(\lambda)P)^{-1}P(J_{VV}(\lambda)P)\right)\otimes\id

\displaystyle=\left((J_{VV}(\lambda)P)^{-1}\otimes\id\right)(P\otimes\id)(\left(J_{VV}(\lambda)P\right)\otimes\id)

\displaystyle=\left((PJ_{VV}(\lambda)P)^{-1}\otimes\id\right)(P\otimes\id)\left(PJ_{VV}(\lambda)P\right)\otimes\id)

\displaystyle=(J^{21}_{VV}(\lambda))^{-1}(P\otimes\id)J^{21}_{VV}(\lambda)

\displaystyle=(J^{3,12}(\lambda)_{V,V\otimes V}J^{21}_{VV}(\lambda))^{-1}(P\otimes\id)J^{3,12}_{V,V\otimes V}(\lambda)J^{21}_{VV}(\lambda)

\displaystyle\id\otimes R_{2}(\lambda-h^{1})

\displaystyle=\id\otimes\left((J_{VV}(\lambda-h^{1})P)^{-1}P(J_{VV}(\lambda-h^{1})P)\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\id\otimes(PJ_{VV}(\lambda-h^{1})P)^{-1}\right)(\id\otimes P)\left(\id\otimes(PJ_{VV}(\lambda-h^{1})P)\right)

\displaystyle=(J^{32}(\lambda-h^{1}))^{-1}(\id\otimes P)J^{32}(\lambda-h^{1})

\displaystyle=\left(J^{23,1}_{V\otimes V,V}(\lambda)J_{VV}^{32}(\lambda-h^{1})\right)^{-1}(\id\otimes P)J^{23,1}_{V\otimes V,V}(\lambda)J_{VV}^{32}(\lambda-h^{1})

We know that
\check{R}_{2}(\lambda)
satisfies the dynamical Yang-Baxter equation.

.

7.
Example: cell system

We first reformulate Ocneanu cell calculus
[
38
,
39
]
and also the cell system introduced in
[
46
]
. The theory of cell calculus is a rather rich theory and we only focus one part of it.

Let
\pi_{1}
and
\pi_{2}
be two finite unoriented graphs with distinguished nodes
*_{1}
and
*_{2}
, with two incidence matrices
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
, we say that there exists a intertwiner from
M_{1}
to
M_{2}
if there exists a
\rm{card}(\operatorname{Ob}(\pi_{1}))\times\rm{card}(\operatorname{Ob}(\pi_{2}))
matrix
C
with nonnegative integer coeffcients, such that:

M_{1}C=CM_{2},\quad C_{*_{1},i}=1\Leftrightarrow i=*_{2},

(132)

and for any
i\in\operatorname{Ob}(\pi_{1})
, there exist
j\in\operatorname{Ob}(\pi_{2})
such that
C_{i,j}\neq 0
, this matrix will give rise to the connecting groupoid, we denote it by
\pi
.

Lemma 7.1
.

There exists a quasi-unique connecting system for
\pi_{2}
.

Proof.

From the condition that
C_{*_{1},i}=1\Leftrightarrow i=*_{2},
for any path
\gamma
in
\pi_{2}
, we can have a path from the starting point
*_{2}
to
s_{\pi}(\gamma)
and the edge
\beta_{1}
which connect
*_{1}
and
*_{2}
satisfies the condition requirement of the quasi-unique connecting system.
∎

The natural
\pi_{1}
graded vector space
V^{\pi_{1}}
is given by

V^{\pi_{1}}_{\alpha}:=\C e_{\alpha}
, that is each edge is associated to a one dimensional vector space, similar for
V^{\pi_{2}}
, suppose that we have an dynamical Yang-Baxter operator
\check{R}_{1}
on
V^{\pi_{1}}
.

For each
left invertible
map
c\in\Gamma(\pi\times\pi,\overline{\operatorname{Hom}}(V^{\pi_{1}},V^{\pi_{2}}))
with its inverse
c^{-1}\in\Gamma(\pi\times\pi,\overline{\operatorname{Hom}}(V^{\pi_{2}},V^{\pi_{1}}))
, let
j=c^{-1}*_{\otimes}c^{-1}\in\Gamma(\pi\times\pi,\overline{\operatorname{Hom}}(V^{\pi_{2}}\otimes V^{\pi_{2}},V^{\pi_{1}}\otimes V^{\pi_{1}}))
, from the lemma
4.9
, it has inverse with
j^{-1}=c^{-1}*_{\otimes}c^{-1}
.

With the map
j
and a chosen quasi-unique connecting system by lemma
7.1
, we can define
\check{R}_{2}\in\Psi\big(\pi\times\pi,\operatorname{End}_{\rhd}(V^{\pi_{2}}\otimes V^{\pi_{2}})\big)

\check{R}_{2}(\beta_{1},\beta_{1})=j^{-1}*_{\circ}(\check{R}_{1}j):=\sum_{\beta}j^{-1}(\beta,\beta_{1})\check{R}_{1}j(\beta_{1},\beta)

(133)

Definition 7.2
.

A
left invertible

c
is called a cell system if for any
\gamma\in\pi_{2}
, we have the following relations

\sum_{\beta}j^{-1}(\beta,\beta_{1})\check{R}_{1}j(\beta_{1},\beta)=\sum_{\beta^{\prime}}j^{-1}(\beta^{\prime},\beta^{\prime}_{1})\check{R}_{1}j(\beta^{\prime}_{1},\beta^{\prime})

(134a)

\sum_{\beta}j^{-1}(\beta,\beta_{2})\check{R}_{1}j(\beta_{1},\beta)=0

(134b)

for any
\beta_{1}
and
\beta^{\prime}_{1}
with
t_{\pi}(\beta^{\prime}_{1})=t_{\pi}(\beta_{1})=s_{\pi_{2}}(\gamma)
, for any
\beta_{2}\neq\beta_{1}
with
s_{\pi}(\beta_{2})=s(\beta_{1})
.

Proposition 7.3
.

Suppose that
c
is invertible, then
c
is a cell twist.

Proof.

As in lemma
7.1
, there always exists a quasi-unique connecting system, the natural choice for any
\gamma
is the path starting from the initial point
*_{2}
to the point
\gamma^{\prime}
, suppose that the length is
n
, then
q
is
n
times
*_{\otimes}
of
c^{-1}
, and the corresponding
q_{1}
is
(n-3)
times
*_{\otimes}
of
c^{-1}
and
q_{2}=c^{-1}*_{\otimes}c^{-1}*_{\otimes}c^{-1}
as in the definition of quasi-unique twist and the relation (
111b
),(
111a
), (
113b
) and (
113a
) follows from (
134
) and the invertibility of c.
∎

Remark 7.4
.

From the definition of cell system, for example in
[
46
]
, the unitary condition in
[
46
]
only implies that
c
is left invertible, but in order to check that it is indeed a twist, we need to check the right invertiblity.

(L-2)_{D}
(L-1)_{D}
=1
(L-2)_{A}
(L-1)_{A}
(L-2)_{D}
(L)_{D}
=-1
(L)_{A}
(L-1)_{A}
(L-2)_{D}
(L-1)_{D}
=1
(L)_{A}
(L-1)_{A}
(L-2)_{D}
(L)_{D}
=1

7.2.
Twist
A_{11}
trigonometric
\check{R}
matrix to trigonometric
E_{6}

This example is a reformulation of Roche’s example of intertwiner in
[

]
, see also the work of Francesco–Zuber
[
11
]
. We got the twist from the intertwiners.

For the restricted dynamical
\check{R}^{\text{ell}}_{A}
matrix defined on the vector space
V^{\pi_{A}}
., we take the trigonometric limit. Let
\langle z\rangle:=\sin(\frac{\pi z}{12})
, then we have the trigonometric
\check{R}^{\rm{tri}}_{A}
matrix (
144
) on the
V^{\pi^{\rm{unres}}_{A}}
, similarly by restriction we get
\check{R}^{\rm{tri}}_{A}
on
V^{\pi_{A}}
.

\begin{split}\check{R}^{\text{tri}}_{A}(z,a)&=\sum_{i=1}^{2}E_{ii}\otimes E_{ii}+\frac{\sqrt{\langle a-1\rangle\langle a+1\rangle}\langle z\rangle}{\langle a\rangle\langle 1-z\rangle}E_{21}\otimes E_{12}+\frac{\sqrt{\langle a+1\rangle\langle a-1\rangle}\langle z\rangle}{\langle a\rangle\langle 1-z\rangle}E_{12}\otimes E_{21}\\
+&\frac{\langle a+z\rangle\langle 1\rangle}{\langle a\rangle\langle 1-z\rangle}E_{11}\otimes E_{22}+\frac{\langle a-z\rangle\langle 1\rangle}{\langle a\rangle\langle 1-z\rangle}E_{22}\otimes E_{11}\end{split}

(146)

We now describe the homomorphism
\widetilde{C}\in\Gamma(\pi\times\pi,\overline{\operatorname{Hom}}(V^{\pi_{A}},V^{\pi_{E}}))
, as the component of
V^{\pi_{A}}
and
V^{\pi_{E}}
are all one dimensional. It suffice to describe the coefficients of the
\widetilde{C}
with respect to these one dimensional basis.

We let
\widetilde{C}(a_{1},a_{2},e_{1},e_{2})
to describe the coefficient of the following component (
147
).

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet_{a_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet_{e_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet_{a_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet_{e_{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V^{\pi_{A}}_{\alpha}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V^{\pi_{E}}_{\gamma}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta_{2}}

(147)

then the
\tilde{C}
is described by

\widetilde{C}(1_{A},2_{A},1_{E},2_{E})=\widetilde{C}(2_{A},1_{A},2_{E},1_{E})=\widetilde{C}(2_{A},3_{A},2_{E},3_{E})=1

\widetilde{C}(3_{A},4_{A},3_{E},4_{E})=\widetilde{C}(3_{A},2_{A},3_{E},2_{E})=-\widetilde{C}(3_{A},4_{A},3_{E},4_{E})=-\widetilde{C}(4_{A},5_{A},4_{E},5_{E})=1

\widetilde{C}(4_{A},3_{A},4_{E},3_{E})=\widetilde{C}(4_{A},5_{A},6_{E},3_{E})=3^{-1/4}

-\widetilde{C}(4_{A},3_{A},6_{E},3_{E})=\widetilde{C}(4_{A},5_{A},4_{E},3_{E})=(1-\frac{1}{\sqrt{3}})^{1/2}

\widetilde{C}(5_{A},4_{A},3_{E},6_{E})=\widetilde{C}(5_{A},6_{A},3_{E},2_{E})=1

-\widetilde{C}(5_{A},4_{A},5_{E},4_{E})=\widetilde{C}(5_{A},6_{A},3_{E},4_{E})=(2\sqrt{3}-3)^{1/2}

\widetilde{C}(5_{A},4_{A},3_{E},4_{E})=\widetilde{C}(5_{A},6_{A},5_{E},4_{E})=\sqrt{3}-1

\widetilde{C}(6_{A},7_{A},2_{E},1_{E})=\widetilde{C}(6_{A},5_{A},4_{E},5_{E})=1

\widetilde{C}(6_{A},5_{A},2_{E},3_{E})=\widetilde{C}(6_{A},7_{A},4_{E},3_{E})=\frac{1}{2}(\sqrt{3}+1)^{1/2}

\widetilde{C}(6_{A},5_{A},4_{E},3_{E})=-\widetilde{C}(6_{A},7_{A},2_{E},3_{E})=\frac{1}{2}(3-\sqrt{3})^{1/2}

\widetilde{C}(7_{A},6_{A},3_{E},4_{E})=\widetilde{C}(7_{A},8_{A},3_{E},6_{E})=1

\widetilde{C}(7_{A},8_{A},1_{E},2_{E})=-\widetilde{C}(7_{A},6_{A},3_{E},2_{E})=(2\sqrt{3}-3)^{1/2}

\widetilde{C}(7_{A},8_{A},3_{E},2_{E})=\widetilde{C}(7_{A},6_{A},1_{E},2_{E})=\sqrt{3}-1

\widetilde{C}(8_{A},7_{A},2_{E},1_{E})=1,\widetilde{C}(8_{A},7_{A},6_{E},3_{E})=\widetilde{C}(8_{A},9_{A},2_{E},3_{E})=3^{-1/4}

-\widetilde{C}(8_{A},9_{A},6_{E},3_{E})=\widetilde{C}(8_{A},7_{A},2_{E},3_{E})=\big(1-\frac{1}{\sqrt{3}}\big)^{1/2}

\widetilde{C}(9_{A},8_{A},3_{E},2_{E})=\widetilde{C}(9_{A},10_{A},3_{E},4_{E})=-\widetilde{C}(9_{A},8_{A},3_{E},6_{E})=1

\widetilde{C}(10_{A},9_{A},4_{E},3_{E})=\widetilde{C}(10_{A},11_{A},4_{E},5_{E})=\widetilde{C}(11_{A},10_{A},5_{E},4_{E})=1

And we also let
(\widetilde{C})^{-1}\in\Gamma(\pi\times\pi,\overline{\operatorname{Hom}}(V^{\pi_{E}},V^{\pi_{A}}))
be described by

Proposition 7.8

(Roche
[
46
]
)
.

\tilde{C}
is a cell system

Lemma 7.9
.

\widetilde{C}
is invertible with the inverse
\widetilde{C}^{-1}
.

Proposition 7.10
.

\tilde{C}
is a cell twist, so from the formula (
134
), we get an
\check{R}^{\rm{ell}}_{D}
defined on the space
V^{\pi_{D}}
.

7.3.
Other cell systems

There are many other important and interesting intertwiners constructed by Pasquier
[
42
]
(this example maybe included as in subsection
6
, see
[
21
]
on discussion about the relation of twist with 3j symbols and 6j symbols), also in Roche
[
46
]
and Francesco–Zuber
[
11
]
. It is not difficult to check that whether these intertwiners are from some cell twists, because the intertwiner relation are proved by the above authors, we only need to check the right invertiblity and also the quasi-unique connecting system condition.
